# Changelog

All notable changes to the Inference compiler project.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Breaking

- **The export descriptor records each parameter's source name.** `ExportSignature::params` — the per-export source-type descriptor `CodegenOutput::export_signatures()` publishes, introduced with the Stellar target in this release — is now a `Vec<AbiParam>`, and `AbiParam { name: Option<String>, ty: AbiType }` pairs each declared type with the name the source wrote: the spelling exactly as written, a leading underscore kept and `mut` dropped, or `None` for a parameter written `_`, which is recorded as unnamed rather than given an invented name. A contract-spec emitter is the consumer it exists for — Stellar's `contractspecv0` describes every input of a method by name, and `stellar contract invoke` turns each name into a `--<name>` flag. The module's `name` custom section records a named parameter only as optional debug data, keyed by function and local index, which an optimizer may strip and which records nothing for `_`; the descriptor is the record a consumer reads. `AbiParam::named` and `AbiParam::unnamed` construct one. The change is byte-neutral: nothing on the emission path reads the descriptor, and every golden is unchanged. Migration: read `param.ty` where a `Vec<AbiType>` element was read; build a literal with `AbiParam::named("a", AbiType::U32)` or `AbiParam::unnamed(ty)`; `params.len()` is unchanged. No tagged release shipped the previous `Vec<AbiType>` shape ([#466])
- **`StellarAbiError` gains `UnnamedParameter`, `ParameterNameTooLong` and `ParameterNamesCollide`, and `AlreadyAContract` now names the section it found.** The three new variants are the rewriter's parameter-name refusals recorded under `### Codegen` below; `AlreadyAContract` becomes `AlreadyAContract { section: String }` because it now refuses three section names rather than one. The enum is not `#[non_exhaustive]`, so an exhaustive `match` over it stops compiling. Migration: add the three arms, and match `AlreadyAContract { .. }` where `AlreadyAContract` was matched. No tagged release shipped the previous shape ([#466])
- **`--target stellar` refuses an exported parameter written `_`, named in more than 30 bytes, or named what another parameter's `stellar` CLI flag alias spells.** A program that built a contract at this target before — `pub fn f(_: u32) -> u32`, and `pub fn f(x: u32, _x: u32) -> u32` or `pub fn f(x: u32, X: u32) -> u32`, among them — no longer does: a contract's `contractspecv0` section describes every parameter by name, in a field of at most 30 bytes, and `stellar contract invoke` takes each argument as a `--<name>` flag built from that name, gives each parameter an alias as well, its name in kebab case, and resolves a flag to whichever parameter claims it first, so a method declaring `x` beside `_x`, `X` or `x_` cannot be called reliably; the source gate in `codegen()` and the Val-ABI rewriter both refuse the export, as the `### CLI` and `### Codegen` bullets below describe. *Unaffected*: other targets; a lone `_amount`-style name, which is kept as written; and names whose aliases merely agree, such as `_x` beside `__x` or `to_addr` beside `toAddr`, each of which keeps a flag of its own (`tests/tests/stellar/MEASURED_ABI.md`). *Migration*: name the parameter, shorten the name, or rename one of the pair. No tagged release shipped the Stellar target ([#466])
- **`unit` is now a reserved word, and the unit type is spelled `()` and nothing else.** The unit type has always been written `()` in the language's grammar; this compiler alone also registered `unit` as a builtin type *name*, so `fn v() -> unit { return; }`, `let d: unit = ();` and `external fn ping() -> unit;` compiled as their `()` forms. None of them compiles now. `unit` lexes as a keyword of its own, and the parser refuses it wherever a type or a declared name is written, with one diagnostic on the word and no cascade after it: where a type is written — a return type, a parameter, binding, constant or field type, an array element, a type argument, an `external fn` signature — it says `` `unit` is a reserved word, not a type: the unit type is spelled `()` ``, and where a name is written it says what the word tried to name, `` `unit` is a reserved word and cannot name a binding `` (or a parameter, field, function, struct, enum, enum variant, constant, spec, type, type alias, type parameter, module or imported item). Where the word is referred to rather than declared — `return unit;`, `s.unit`, `unit::k()`, `unit();` — the syntax does not say what the name names, so it says `` `unit` is a reserved word and cannot be used as a name ``, and the word is consumed as that name all the same, so a declaration and a use of it are two diagnostics and nothing else. All three are parse errors, so they are fatal in every mode, and nothing downstream of a successful parse sees the word; an editor, which analyses a file with syntax errors, still does. This withdraws two earlier entries in this release: the widening, recorded under `### Codegen`, that made `-> unit` compile as a return type, and the lowering, recorded under `### Compiler`, that gave an `external fn` declared `-> unit` an empty result list — neither spelling reaches those phases any more. Because the word cannot name anything either, a source file `unit.inf` cannot be imported, a host import cannot be bound from a module or under a field named `unit`, and `infs new unit` is refused as a reserved project name. An identifier that merely begins with the word — `units`, `unit_count` — is a single token and is unaffected. The two fixtures that spelled a return type `unit` — a `void_forms` code generation golden and a `panic_free` shape — which existed only to pin the `unit` spelling, are deleted with it, and a `-> ()` fixture takes the place of each; no other committed `.wasm`, `.wat` or `.v` golden moves. Migration: write `()` wherever the type was spelled `unit`, and rename anything named `unit`; a host or library export named `unit` has no spelling that binds it, since a `use … from` clause has no `as`, so it must be renamed or re-exported under another name by whoever provides it
- **`Inference.toml` gains a root table, `[host-imports]`, which an `infs` that predates it cannot honor — and the older the `infs`, the quieter the failure.** An `infs` that has the unknown-key refusal but not the table refuses to load a manifest carrying it, naming the key: loud, and fixed by upgrading. An `infs` from before that refusal — every release through `v0.0.5` — reads past the table with no diagnostic and builds under no policy at all, which is the allowlist dropped silently, the one outcome the table exists to prevent; the only trace is the `(no allowlist)` qualifier on the `host imports:` line `infc` prints. A project that relies on its allowlist has to be built with an `infs` that knows the table. *Migration*: none for a manifest without the table ([#464])
- **The compiler ABI minor is now 8; a tool that holds a host-import policy must gate `--host-imports` on it and refuse rather than drop the flag.** Minor 8 is additive like the seven before it — omitting the flag applies no policy, which is what every earlier minor did with a host binding, so a minor-7 `infs` still pairs with a minor-8 `infc`. A minor-7 `infs` loses nothing by that, because it cannot hold a policy: it predates the `Inference.toml [host-imports]` table and refuses a manifest carrying that key. It is recorded here rather than beside the other minor bumps because the pairing the gate exists for — an `infs` that holds a policy, talking to a minor-7 `infc` — loses something no later inspection can recover. A dropped `--target` leaves a wrong artifact or a conformance check that visibly never ran; a dropped `--host-imports` leaves an artifact identical, byte for byte, to one a policy admitted, asking an embedder for functions the project never admitted. Nothing in the bytes records it, so there is no wrong artifact to find later; the whole of the signal is the `(no allowlist)` qualifier on the `host imports:` line the build prints, a line only a program that binds at least one host import produces at all, and it is gone the moment the build's output is. Gating on the minor is what keeps that from being a reviewer's job. The empty spelling is sharper still: `--host-imports=` is a policy that admits nothing, and a caller that decides the flag is unnecessary turns a project declaring it binds no host functions at all into one that binds whatever its source happens to say. A forward that *reaches* a minor-7 `infc` fails loudly on a flag it does not parse, so the only unsafe move is omitting it. This supersedes the `[#325]` `### CLI` bullet below where it records that `infc --abi-version` prints `1.7`, which is left as written per this file's append-and-leave practice. *Migration*: none for a build; a tool that holds a host-import policy and forwards it to `infc` must gate `--host-imports` on minor 8 and refuse rather than drop it ([#464])
- **A `--wasm-dep` name or an `Inference.toml [wasm-dependencies]` key whose first segment is `host` is now a hard error.** A dependency key names the logical module a `use … from` clause refers to, and `host` is reserved as the first segment there for imports the embedder supplies, so no linked module can be named under it. Previously `--wasm-dep host=io.wasm` bound an ordinary module — and took precedence over every search directory — which is the provider substitution the reservation exists to make impossible, reached through the one door that skips the search path entirely. `infs` refuses such a `[wasm-dependencies]` key itself, in the manifest's own terms — ``invalid [wasm-dependencies] key `host::a`: `host` is reserved as the first segment of a `use … from` clause for imports the embedder supplies, so no linked module can be named under it`` — when it resolves the table to forward it, on every build and run route and before `infc` is asked to build, with `infc`'s two remedies, its closing migration clause, and the one remedy only a project has: list the function under `[host-imports]` if the project keeps an allowlist. `infc`'s own refusal is therefore the one a direct `infc` invocation meets, or a key forwarded by an `infs` that predates that check; it opens ``invalid --wasm-dep `host=io.wasm`: …`` — the flag and the whole entry, as its two sibling refusals do, since a manifest key arrives here without saying where it came from — and states two remedies rather than one, because it is the only one of the three that can fire on a program whose *source* is already correct: an entry written for a host import is simply deleted — a `use … from host::<module>;` clause needs no dependency entry at all, since the embedder supplies the function and no file is named — and the rename remedy the two source-level `host`-clause refusals end with is kept, worded conditionally, for the reader who did mean a linked module. Issued unconditionally it would tell an author with a working `use { clock_ms } from host::env;` and a `host::env` dependency entry beside it to turn the working binding into a linked module, which is the provider substitution the reservation exists to prevent. *Unaffected*: the match is exact and on the first segment alone, on both sides, so `hostlib`, `Host` and `a::host` still bind — `--wasm-dep a::host=io.wasm` names an ordinary linked module. *Migration*: rename the module — the `.wasm` file or directory, the `[wasm-dependencies]` key or `--wasm-dep` name, and the `use … from` clause that binds it — out of that first segment ([#464])
- **`host` is now reserved as the first segment of a `use … from` clause.** `use { telemetry } from host::fprime_core;` declares that `telemetry` is supplied by the *embedder* at run time rather than by a `.wasm` file this build links, and the single segment after `host` is the WebAssembly import module string an embedder registers — `fprime_core`, never `host::fprime_core`, because `host` names the provider and not a component of the module's name. It is a position rather than a keyword: `host` stays an ordinary identifier everywhere else, `host::host` legally imports from a module named `host`, and the match is exact, so `Host::env` is still an ordinary linked module. What breaks is any linked `.wasm` module whose `from` clause *opens* with `host`, and it breaks in two ways. `from host;` and `from host::a::b;` are refused outright and the refusals say why; because the linked module the second one used to bind is `host::a::b` — the file `host/a/b.wasm`, or that manifest key — its remedy is to **rename** that module, not to drop the `host` segment, which would name a different module that does not exist. The other way is a silent *reinterpretation*: `from host::io;`, which used to link `host/io.wasm` and emit `(import "host::io" …)`, is now a host import of the module `io`. *Migration*: rename any linked module whose logical path starts with `host` — the `.wasm` file or directory, its `[wasm-dependencies]` key, and the `from` clause — out of that first segment, and do it before the next build, because afterwards the only build-log trace is the `host imports (no allowlist): …` line a successful build prints (see the `### CLI` entry), and a program that also links a module is refused without naming the clause. There is no escape spelling: `host::host` is a host import of a module named `host`, not a linked one. That migration is the only protection there is, which is why it leads rather than follows: nothing diagnoses the reinterpretation, in this cycle or any later one — the clause is byte-for-byte the spelling a correct host binding takes, so the front end has nothing to object to — and the rest of the pipeline now carries a host import end to end, so the build *succeeds*. `from host::io;` resolves with no search directory probed, `host/io.wasm` is left unread on disk, and the artifact ships `(import "io" "f" …)` for an embedder to satisfy, with no build diagnostic — `infs run` refuses the program, naming `io.f`. That holds end to end only where the reinterpreted clause is the program's *sole* extern. A project that had a `host/io.wasm` dependency very likely binds other libraries too, and one that also binds a linked module is instead **refused**, by the mixed-program error in the `### Compiler` entry below — so the build stops, but it stops naming a *host import* the author never wrote, alongside linked modules that are not what went wrong; to a reader who does not know about the reservation that refusal reads as an unrelated failure in a program with no host bindings at all. Either way the rename is the fix, and either way nothing names the clause that changed meaning. The two malformed shapes above are the only `host`-clause refusals there are, and the rename is their remedy as well as this one's. Both malformed shapes are refused once for the *directive*, never once per imported field: `use { a, b, c } from host;` reports one ``names no import module`` and drops all three fields, so none of them is additionally reported as a dangling import contradicting it, while the file's other, well-formed directives still bind. A linked and a host module that reduce to the same string (`from a;` and `from host::a;`) stay two bindings rather than silently collapsing. When one file's clauses bind *one name* both ways, that is the existing ambiguity error, whose text changes for *every* ambiguous extern: the rule it states is now one **binding** rather than ``exactly one module``, which was false of precisely this pair, and a list in which a linked clause and a host one reduce to one module string gains a sentence saying what the two spellings differ in — a list of two genuinely different names (`` `a` `` and `` `host::b` ``) keeps the shorter message, since there the names explain themselves. Reaching one import module from both kinds by any other route — two files, or two different fields in one file — is a new refusal naming both clauses and both files, because an import module has one provider: the linker's candidate set for any import is every export of a `.wasm` module bound under that module string, so a merged library would be offered to satisfy the very import its sibling left for an embedder, and an embedder registers a module as a whole rather than a function at a time. Where the two clauses also name one field, code generation additionally folds them onto one `(import "a" "f" …)` entry that cannot be both linked away and left for an embedder. The specification translation refuses a host extern as an obligation's subject — in term position and as a bare call statement alike — since no body of it is ever spliced into the artifact an obligation is about; the *unbound* extern's existing `P005` refusal is reworded in the same breath, from ``… so no module supplies the body an obligation about it would reduce`` to ``… so this build names no module at all to take the body an obligation about it would reduce from``, because the two now have to say which of the three states they are about — so a test or an embedder matching the old unbound wording has to be updated even though nothing about unbound externs changed. Emission is unchanged: a host-bound extern emits the same `(import "<module>" "<field>" …)` entry a linked one does, and every pre-existing golden module is byte-identical. The rest of the pipeline carries the binding rather than guessing at a file for it: the external-module driver resolves a `from host::…` binding without probing the search path at all, so a same-named `.wasm` — for `env`, the canonical host-module name, a likely rather than exotic collision — is never linked in and the import is never stripped. What the driver and the link step then do with it is the `### Compiler` entry below ([#464])
- **All three of `codegen()`'s configuration refusals — proof mode, a post-MVP feature request, a non-deterministic function — now render the target as the name `--target` accepts, not as its Rust `Debug` spelling.** `Target::as_str()` is what the three messages interpolate, so the Stellar target reads as `stellar` rather than `Stellar`. The Stellar export-admissibility refusals are unchanged and still open `Stellar target: …`, which is prose rather than an interpolated variant name. This is not tidiness: two of the three end in an instruction — build at a different target, drop a requested feature — and a reader who pastes back what the message printed has to get a name the flag resolves. Two of the three also changed past the rendering, and both changes are the actionable sentence each was missing. A `proof`-mode build now reads ``Proof mode requires the `wasm32` target. Proof mode emits custom 0xfc non-deterministic instructions that only `wasm32` accepts; the `stellar` runtime rejects a module carrying them. Build the proof at `--target wasm32`: code generation is target-blind, so the module a `stellar` build starts from is the `wasm32` build's.`` A non-deterministic function now reads ``The `stellar` target does not support non-deterministic operations. Function 'f' contains non-deterministic constructs (uzumaki, forall, exists, assume, or unique blocks), which compile to custom 0xfc WebAssembly instructions the `stellar` runtime rejects. Non-deterministic code is specification code: move it into a `spec` block, which compile mode strips from the artifact, or build this program with `--target wasm32`.`` — without that last sentence the refusal reads as "this target cannot run Inference programs", which is the opposite of true. The bulk-memory refusal changes in its two target renderings only: ``The `stellar` target does not support the 'bulk-memory' WebAssembly feature. … drop 'bulk-memory' from the requested features to build for `stellar`.`` This moves the Stellar texts a second time in one unreleased cycle and **supersedes** the `[#324]` `Target::Soroban` → `Target::Stellar` bullet below, which quotes the wording these replace; that bullet is left as written, per this file's append-and-leave practice. Migration: a test asserting on any of the three whole strings has to be updated — and a substring assertion that omitted the target name keeps passing, so it is now weaker than it looks ([#325])
- `check_wasm1` moves out of `core/stellar-abi` into the new `core/target-conformance`, keeping its `Result<(), String>` shape so `StellarAbiError::{InputNotWasm1, RewrittenNotWasm1}` stay byte-identical. Two targets are now held to the WebAssembly 1.0 envelope, and two crates spelling it independently is how the two answers drift apart; `inference-stellar-abi` and `inference-cli` call the one definition. **`inference_stellar_abi::check_wasm1` is gone as a re-export** — the crate documents where the question lives instead of forwarding it, so there is one path to it rather than two. Migration: call `inference_target_conformance::check_wasm1` and depend on `inference-target-conformance`, which is a true leaf and pulls in nothing the caller did not already need ([#325])
- **`inference_wasm_codegen::Target::Soroban` is renamed to `Target::Stellar`.** The enum variant is the only public item that moves, but all three of `codegen()`'s target-naming refusals change their user-visible text with it, one of them further than a rename, and a coverage mark changes name too — none of which a variant rename implies. Two of `codegen()`'s refusals render the target through `{target:?}`, so their user-visible text changes with no format string in the source having contained the old spelling: a bulk-memory request now reads `Stellar target does not support the 'bulk-memory' WebAssembly feature. …`, and a `proof`-mode build now reads `… the Stellar target cannot process these.` A third message change rides along, and it is not a rename: the bulk-memory refusal's middle sentence used to say the target's runtime `is not known to accept those instructions, so a module using them may be rejected at deployment`, which is not why the request is refused — that runtime's documented wasmi configuration enables the proposal, and the refusal is a deliberate pin to the WebAssembly 1.0 instruction set, taken because no lowering the target can reach needs the instructions and a build-time refusal is a better failure than a deploy-time one. It now reads `This target is pinned to the WebAssembly 1.0 instruction set, so a module using the instructions 'bulk-memory' adds is refused here rather than at deployment; drop 'bulk-memory' from the requested features to build for Stellar.` The remaining refusal, which spelled the name out in full, now reads `Stellar target does not support non-deterministic operations. …`, and its coverage mark `wasm_codegen_soroban_rejects_nondet_function` is now `wasm_codegen_stellar_rejects_nondet_function` — a `cov_mark` name is matched across crates at run time, so a `check!` left on the old name panics rather than failing to compile. `wasm_codegen_target_rejects_feature` is deliberately untouched: it never named a target. The two Codegen entries below that describe the old name — the one introducing `Target` (Wasm32/Soroban) and the one adding the "reject Soroban with non-det operations" guard — are superseded by this bullet and are left as written, since nothing ever shipped under the old name. Migration: rename `Target::Soroban` to `Target::Stellar`, and rename any `cov_mark::check!` on the old mark ([#324])
- **`Inference.toml`'s `[build] target` is now validated on load, and a value outside the supported set fails the build.** The key was previously accepted whatever it said: `target = "wasm64"` loaded, was never read, and produced an ordinary `wasm32` artifact with nothing anywhere reporting that the project had named a target that does not exist. It is now resolved against the same vocabulary `infc --target` uses, and rejected with the same wording — Invalid `[build] target` value `wasm64`: unknown compilation target. Supported targets: `wasm32`. — before any build work runs. Matching is exact and case-sensitive with no whitespace trimmed, so `"Wasm32"` and `"wasm32 "` are refused too, the latter with a sentence naming the space as the cause, since a trailing space inside a TOML string is invisible in the echoed value. The supported set holds one name today, so the only manifests this breaks are ones declaring a target that never worked. Migration: write `target = "wasm32"`, or delete the key ([#324])
- **`checked` and `wrapping` are now reserved words.** They are the keywords of the two arithmetic-mode expression forms below, and unlike `self`, `type`, `from` and `spec` they are not admitted in identifier position, so every name spelled either way is now a parse error: a variable, a `const` or a function name (`let checked: i32 = 1;`, `const wrapping: i32 = 1;`, `pub fn wrapping(x: i32) -> i32 { ... }`), a struct field and the access that reads it, a method and the call that invokes it, an enum variant, and a segment of a `use` path — which takes the source file stem with it, since a file is compiled only when some `use` names it, so a `wrapping.inf` still parses on its own and nothing can import it. Previously each of those was an ordinary identifier that compiled and ran. The word is classified in the lexer rather than at the position it stands in, so the reservation is unconditional and the report is a parse error at the name itself — `expected an identifier`, joined, where a `(` follows the word, by the sentence that explains the expression form — and `parse()` returns `Err` as soon as any diagnostic is recorded, so it is fatal in both compilation modes and nothing downstream receives the name at all. Unaffected: an identifier that merely contains one of the words is a single token and still compiles, so `checked_sum`, `wrapping_add` and `unchecked` are untouched, as is either word inside a string or a comment; no in-tree program had to be edited, because across the whole `.inf` corpus the two words occur only inside one fixture comment, which the reservation does not reach. Migration: rename the identifier ([#316])
- **`+`, `-`, `*` and unary `-` now trap when the result leaves the operand type.** An operator no `wrapping(...)` encloses computes a result its type cannot hold by trapping through `unreachable`, at every integer width and both signednesses, in compile and proof builds alike; previously every one of them produced the two's-complement result instead, and nothing reported it. `wrapping(e)` restores that arithmetic for every `+`, `-`, `*` and unary `-` written between its parentheses and is the only way to ask for it: which operator `a + b` denotes is a property of the language, so no flag, manifest key, build profile or compilation mode turns the trap off, and one source still compiles to one program. `/`, `%`, the shifts and the bitwise operators are still governed by neither spelling and behave exactly as they did. An operation whose operands are known before the program runs and whose result leaves its type is now refused outright rather than compiled into a trap taken on every run that reaches it (**A052**, error), unless it is written `wrapping(...)`. Two consequences reach past the expression. A specification that claims a call is realized now claims that call does not overflow, which is what the change is for — but by the same token an `exists`- or `unique`-quantified body may not carry a trapping operator or reach one through a call, so a reachability specification stated over ordinary arithmetic code is refused (`P017`, `P018`) until that code says it is modular. And a library optimized under `[build.wasm-opt]` now carries the opaque form of its overflow-guard record whenever any of its arithmetic is unmarked, because the optimizer renumbers the functions an exact record names — a project linking it may still state `forall` specifications about it and may no longer state a reachability one. Unaffected: division and remainder, which neither spelling governs and whose behaviour is unchanged, `MIN % -1` still `0` at every width; the bytes of any expression written `wrapping(...)`, which are the bytes that expression compiled to before guards existed; and the run-time behaviour of every program whose arithmetic cannot leave its type, which is most of them. Migration: mark the sites that mean to wrap. This repository's own corpus is the worked example — twenty-two fixtures took a `wrapping(...)` and five specifications gained or tightened an `assume` envelope instead — and the shapes it turned up are the ones to look for: a specification whose claim *is* the wrapping contract, such as `diff_i32(a, b) == a - b`, where the callee's operator takes the mark; a retained `exists`/`unique` body and everything it reaches, which has to be modular throughout or the rules above refuse it; an accumulator whose last iteration computes a value nobody reads, such as the final squaring of an exponentiation loop; a probe that reads its parameters by summing them; a `const` folded into an operator at the boundary of its type; and any hash, mixer or pseudo-random generator, which is wrapping by construction — the splitmix64 in the raytracer benchmark, which is not in this repository, is three sites and breaks until they are marked. Where the arithmetic is meant to stay inside its type the other remedy applies: state the range as an `assume` envelope over the operands and let the guard be the thing the proof rules out ([#316])
- **`type` aliases are removed from the language.** Both forms go — the item `[pub] type A = i32;` and the body-level `type A = i32;` written inside a function block or a `spec` — and the parser refuses the declaration outright, saying: type aliases are not supported; an alias declares a name no value can ever have, so the declaration can only ever be unused — write the type it names at each use site: `let n: i32 = 1;` rather than `type N = i32; let n: N = 1;`. Nothing that worked is taken away, because an alias was uninhabited: nothing ever resolved an alias name back to the type it named, so no value could be produced at that name, and a use of one in a position that has to be lowered — a signature, a struct field — was refused by code generation rather than compiled. What did compile was the declaration nobody could use, contributing no instruction and consuming no local. The refusal is made at the parser, so it is fatal in both compilation modes and nothing downstream can receive an alias at all: `parse()` returns `Err` as soon as any diagnostic is recorded. The rule reports without recovering, because `type` is itself an item-recovery anchor — skipping to the `;` would consume nothing and let the top-level advance guard fire a second, bogus "expected an item", while bumping the keyword first would abandon at the `fn` of `type Op = fn(i32) -> i32;` — so reporting inside the intact production is what keeps one alias to exactly one diagnostic and lets the item after it parse untouched. The representation goes with it: `Def::TypeAlias` and `Stmt::TypeDef`, the type checker's three registration paths (including the one that registers a definition loaded from an external module), the definition-value graph's alias edge, A034's `pub type`-inside-a-`spec` case, and the alias arm of code generation's signature lowering together with the repair advice it built — so A051's message no longer names a type alias among the declarations that take no type parameters, and a value cycle is now a cycle among `const` initializers and nothing else. **`type` stays a contextual keyword**: it is still a name wherever the grammar reaches its identifier rule, so `fn type() -> i32 { return 1; }` and `let type: i32 = 5;` both compile, as does `self.type` in member position. The two identifier positions that refuse it — a parameter name and a struct-field name — refuse every contextual keyword and did so before this change. The one place the spelling cannot stand is the head of a statement, where the leading-keyword dispatch reads `type();` as a declaration rather than as a call — unchanged by this entry, but the reason the new diagnostic is gated on the declaration's `type <name>` head, so a statement that reaches the rule for any other reason is not blamed on an alias. `Symbol::TypeAlias` stays in the symbol table as the binding every builtin type (`i32`, `bool`, …) is registered under — neither the token nor that binding is a declaration a user can write. The two `void_forms` code generation fixtures that existed only to pin that an alias statement emits nothing are deleted with their subject; no other committed `.wasm`, `.wat` or `.v` golden moves. Migration: write the type the alias named at each use site, and delete the declaration ([#182])
- The WASM `name` section is now two disjoint namespaces rather than one shared by two producers. A compiled function's symbol is its **file-qualified** name — a non-entry-file `add` in `lib/arith.inf` is `lib.arith.add`, a method is `lib.geo.Point.dist` — while a merged external body is `::`-joined: `mathlib::double` for a satisfied import, `mathlib::#helper` for a private callee of the linked module, `mathlib::#func_<idx>` for one whose module carried no name section. The two cannot meet, because every component of a compiled name is an Inference identifier and the joiner is `.`, so a program name can never contain a `:`. Previously both producers composed over the same `.`, and a project with a source module `mathlib` beside a linked logical module `mathlib` could emit an obligation about the **external's** body while the source named the program's own function — a false claim shipping as a true, dischargeable obligation at exit 0. `FnKey::Display` itself is unchanged and still drives diagnostics, panic text and the codegen↔analysis interchange map — but `.wat` output is not a `Display` consumer, it renders the name-section symbol verbatim, and that symbol is exactly what moved: 12 of the 25 changed goldens are `.wat` files (`two_file.wat`'s `(func $helper …)` becomes `(func $util.helper …)`), the other 13 the `.wasm` bytes carrying the same rename. Specification functions keep their bare symbols by deliberate carve-out (spec membership already travels as indices in `inference.spec_funcs`), so a spec's own emitted `Definition` name is unaffected — but that carve-out is not why no `.v` golden in this repo moves; every corpus `.v` fixture happens to be single-file. A **non-entry-file program function**'s `Definition` name is file-qualified right along with its `.wasm` symbol: a `lib/arith.inf` `add` compiled in proof mode now emits `Definition lib_arith_add`, not `Definition add`. Migration: a downstream Rocq proof naming a non-entry-file function's `Definition` must be re-pinned to its new, file-qualified identifier. As a direct consequence a **cross-file** `T_app` symbol now resolves, retiring the limitation the previous entry pinned ([#362])
- wasm-linker: the merge now refuses to emit a module whose proof obligations do not resolve. A function symbol an obligation applies that no function of the output carries is `LinkError::UnresolvedObligationSymbol`, naming the imports the merge did satisfy; one that two functions carry is `LinkError::AmbiguousObligationSymbol`, saying where each came from. Both are checked in the linker rather than in `wasm-to-v` because the merge writes no import section, so afterwards nothing knows which declaration asked for which body. An export bound under two `external fn` declarations is no longer last-write-wins either: one name is recorded (the least, so import order does not decide it) and an obligation over the other is rewritten onto it, where before the earlier alias named nothing at all. Where that unrecorded name is one some other function of the output already carries, the obligation names two bodies at once and is `LinkError::AmbiguousObligationSymbol` too — reported rather than silently read as either the merged body or the function the name section records, since one of those two readings is a false claim shipping as a true, dischargeable obligation. A collision no obligation applies still links, because nothing else in the artifact resolves a function by name ([#362])
- `mut` on an `external fn` parameter is no longer inert: it now declares that the foreign body may **store** through the address that parameter denotes, and the linker checks the claim against the merged bytes, rejecting a body that stores where its declaration did not permit with new `LinkError::UndeclaredExternWrite`. Previously the marker parsed and lowered (`is_mut: true` on the declaration's `ArgKind`) but changed nothing — measured byte-identical `.wasm` and `.v` for scalar, struct and array parameters at every prior commit. New analysis rule **A047** requires a *compound* (struct or array) argument at a `mut` position to be rooted at a `mut` binding, the same requirement an ordinary assignment through that binding already carries; a *scalar* argument is out of A047's scope; it passes a value rather than a region, so there is no binding at the call site for the rule to check — an accepted gap, not an absence of risk, closed only by the containment analysis [#420] tracks. `mut` is required, and now checked, on **every** parameter a writing external's body stores through, not only compound ones: the raw `i32` pointer of the documented `external fn store_at(ptr: i32, val: i32);` idiom is exactly the shape the contract exists to cover, so it must become `store_at(mut ptr: i32, val: i32)`. **This removes a capability**: an immutable compound parameter or `self` receiver may no longer be forwarded to a `mut` external position — the three [#220]/[#329] acceptance fixtures that did so (`fn touch(self)`, `fn touch_param(p: Pair)`, `fn touch_array(a: [i32; 2])`) are now A047 errors once the external they call declares a `mut` parameter, which is correct: `self`'s or the parameter's value does change across such a call, and the source never said it could. **The unnamed parameter forms are deprecated for a writing external**: `ArgKind::Ignored`/`TypeOnly` carry no mutability field and the grammar has no slot for one, so `external fn sort_pair([i32; 2]);` bound to a library that stores through its parameter has an empty declared write set by construction and cannot link; `UndeclaredExternWrite`'s message teaches the only fix available — name the parameter, then declare it `mut`. Two bound declarations of one `(module, field)` whose `mut` masks disagree are now a hard `ExternalResolutionError::ConflictingWriteSet`, naming both files, since the linker checks the merged body once and cannot honor two different declarations of it. Migration: add `mut` to every parameter a linked external's body stores through, including scalar ones, and stop forwarding an immutable compound binding to a position that needs it — bind it `mut` first, or copy it into a `mut` local and pass that ([#333])
- An `external fn`'s identity is now the **declaration** (the declaring `DefId`), resolved in the scope the call is written in — the declaring file, and the `spec` block within it — rather than the bare name looked up program-wide. Two shapes now compile that did not: two files may each declare `external fn f` and bind it to a different module, with each file's calls reaching its own binding; and a file's own bound extern is no longer broken by an unrelated same-named declaration elsewhere in the program. Two now error that did not: a `use … from` clause no longer reaches across files to bind another file's declaration, so one naming an extern its own file does not declare is the dangling-import error `ExternImportNotDeclared`; and a free function and an `external fn` sharing one bare name is the new `ExternFunctionNameCollision`, whether the two sit in one file or in different files: one bare name may not denote both a local function and an external declaration in one program. Each call in such a program resolves — to the declaration its own file names — and the pair is rejected as a rule about the spelling, because a call site does not say whether its callee is compiled here or linked in. `AmbiguousExternModule` is correspondingly a within-file rule — one name, one module, per file — and `extern_origins()` deduplicates on `(logical_module, export_field, decl)` rather than on `(module, field)`, so every declaration is still validated against the library's signature instead of only the first per `(module, field)` pair. Migration: rename either half of a colliding free-function/`external fn` pair, and give each file the `use … from` clause binding its own declaration; a program that does not reuse an extern name across files is unaffected ([#423])
- A specification may now call a **bound** `external fn`, so a linked external `.wasm` is reasonable about in proof mode: the call becomes a `T_app`/`HA_app_ok` naming the body the static-merge linker splices in (`mathlib::double` for `use { double } from mathlib;`), which is the symbol the merge writes into the name section, and `wasm-to-v` resolves it against the **linked** module. Previously every extern call in a spec was **P005** ("external functions carry no verified body"), which made the linker envelope unusable for its stated purpose — an external could be linked and executed but nothing could be claimed about it. The declaration is resolved by `DefId`, not by name: two `external fn`s may share a name across scopes (a bound one at file scope, an unbound one inside a `spec`) and only the declaration the call site actually names decides whether it has a linked body, so a name-keyed lookup would hand a spec-inner declaration the top-level one's origin and name a merged body the call does not reach (`A024` resolves unbound-extern calls through the same scope walk and rejects such a call first, so this is defense in depth everywhere except a pipeline that skips analysis — which the proof-mode test gates are). An **unbound** extern keeps **P005**, with a message naming the missing `use … from` binding rather than the extern; through `infc`/`infs` that message is not what a user sees, since **A024** fires on the same call first — P005 is what an embedder or an analysis-skipping pipeline gets. Two `wasm-to-v` rejections come with it, both of which could previously produce a well-formed but meaningless `.v`: an obligation whose application arity differs from the resolved function's parameter count is now a hard error (`T_app`'s arguments are a `seq term`, so a wrong-width application elaborates and passes `coqc` while describing a different function), and an obligation naming a function the module *imports* now says the merge has not run instead of reporting the symbol as unknown. Unaffected: every obligation that names no extern is byte-identical, and no committed `.wasm`/`.wat`/`.v` golden moves ([#363])
- Proof mode now compiles `exists`- and `unique`-quantified spec functions end to end instead of rejecting them as **P001**. The body is *reachability-lowered*: compiled to vanilla WASM with one hidden trailing choice parameter per scalar `@` (name-section label `__choice{k}`; narrow-type domain normalization kept) and `assume`/`assert` as trap-on-false filters; the function is *retained* in the emitted `.v` module record — `forall`/plain spec functions stay omitted, and every executable reference to a retained function is rejected (it is the subject of a judgment, not a callable) — and its obligation is emitted as a `reachability_spec` record (`reach_func`/`reach_entry_arity`/`reach_visible_locs`/`reach_payload`) under a kind-selected theorem: `ValidExistsSpec` for `exists`, `ValidUniqueSpec` for `unique`, with the preamble import gaining ` Exists` only in reachability-bearing modules. The payload binds each `@` to its choice parameter's frame slot — no `HA_ex` binder, no `HA_has_type` guard: the judgment runs the retained body, so the frame supplies value and typing — while `_specs`/`ValidSpec` stays unconditional per spec name and forall-only output is byte-identical. The quantifier kind travels in `inference.hspecs` wire **v2** (per-entry kind byte plus reachability metadata; encode is always v2, decode rejects v1 — the section is proof-mode intermediate data, so recompile rather than migrate). **P001** narrows to standalone `assume` bodies, with a message explaining why an `assume` body states no property; new fatal diagnostics **P011** (any spec body calling an `exists`/`unique` spec function — the callee's compiled form carries hidden choice parameters no call site supplies) and **P012** (an anonymous `@` in a `unique` body — a choice nothing names cannot distinguish source-visible exit states; bind it first with `let c: i32 = @;`). Compound `@` in a reachability body keeps **P008** with a reachability-specific message; reachability bodies must be void and `return`-free (contract-forced: the downstream judgment reduces the retained body without an activation frame, so a `return` could never take a step), enforced by a codegen pre-scan even on analysis-skipping pipelines. `unique` compares source-visible exit states — entry parameters plus named `let x = @` choices, declared in `reach_visible_locs`; a `unique`-quantified *body* is a deliberate extension ahead of the language-spec amendment, while nested `unique` *blocks* stay **P002**. Reachability translation hard-depends on the WASM name section, and the obligations are dischargeable only for import-free modules (the always-link pipeline satisfies this). Also fixes the A002/A005 message article (`a 'exists' block` → `an 'exists' block`). Full contract in `core/wasm-to-v/ROCQ_CONTRACT.md` ([#354])
- New fatal proof-mode diagnostic **P010**: a spec function whose obligation collapses to the vacuous `HA_true` now aborts code generation instead of being emitted. Such an obligation is discharged by any proof without reading the program, so a green `Qed` over it carried zero verification content and no warning said so — `spec Caller { fn caller() -> i32 { return helper(); } }` emitted `Definition …_hspec1 : hassert := HA_true.` and the meaning the author wrote ("caller equals helper") was simply dropped. The check is the translated result, not the body shape: `HAssert::and`/`imp`/`or`/`ex` absorb `⊤`, so every vacuity path collapses to exactly `HA_true` and one equality catches them all — an empty or assert-free body, a body that only computes (`return`, pure `let`/`const`), a trailing `assume` (`Imp(p, ⊤) = ⊤`), and an `if` whose branches all vacuate, several of which look like they contribute. The message names what the body claimed and what to write instead. A spec function may still `return` — a body that also asserts is unaffected. Consequence: a helper that only computes can no longer live inside a `spec` block; move it to file scope, where a spec function still applies it as a `T_app`. A plain spec *method* keeps its helper exemption because it produces no obligation either way, so the same helper wrapped in a spec-inner struct still compiles — but a plain method that *states* a property — one carrying an `assert` at any depth, a non-deterministic block that asserts nothing excluded — now raises **P009** rather than being dropped with no output at all, which is the widening that closes the worst silent path in the area. Unaffected: compile mode, which has no obligations; every spec function that already stated a property, whose obligation is byte-identical. Migration: give the spec function an `assert`, or move it out of the `spec` block ([#356])
- Proof-mode obligations now encode a term-position `&&`/`||` as an `HA_ex`-bound witness pinned by a two-armed constraint — `Hor (nz l ∧ v = 1) (eqz l ∧ … ∧ v = r)` for `||`, dually for `&&` — instead of an eager `T_binop … BOI_and`/`BOI_or`. The eager term demanded that both operands denote, which neither the source language nor code generation does: `&&`/`||` short-circuit, and `lower_short_circuit_binary` emits a valued `if` precisely so a skipped right operand cannot trap. wasm-verifier's `term_denote` is strict in both `T_binop` operands, so `assert((x == 0 || 10 / x == 10 / x) == true);` — true for every `i32`, and returning normally under Wasmtime — emitted an obligation that is provably false at `x = 0`, where the division does not denote. Every term position was affected, not just a comparison operand: a pure `let` of a `bool`, a `const` initializer, an `if` condition, a call argument. The witness constraint mirrors the compiled control flow exactly, and a constraint belonging to a right operand is planted in the arm that evaluates it rather than hoisted to the statement's atom, so nesting a short-circuit inside another one does not re-introduce the eager demand one level up. This also removes a divergence the eager shape had independently of partiality: a `bool` slot is guarded only `HA_has_type … T_i32` and so may hold any `i32`, where the program computes `1 && 2` as `2` but the eager term computed `1 & 2` as `0`. Unaffected: `&&`/`||` in assertion position already lowered to `HA_and`/`Hor` faithfully and are byte-identical, as is every obligation containing no term-position `&&`/`||` — both committed `.v` goldens are unchanged. One accepted-program narrowing comes with it: a boolean chain used to live in the term tree's own depth budget, which restarts at each assertion atom, and now costs assertion-spine levels instead — two per operator written left-associatively (`a && b && c`), four when parenthesised to the right. A chain of roughly 250 operators used to compile; the limits are now about 100 left-associated and 63 right-nested, past which the existing `CodegenError::HspecTreeTooDeep` rejects the spec by name rather than truncating it. Downstream proofs over a previously emitted term-position `&&`/`||` must be restated against the witness shape ([#383])
- Argument labels at a call site are now checked instead of discarded: mixing named and positional arguments in one call fails as `MixedNamedAndPositionalArguments`, a label naming no parameter of the callee fails as `UnknownArgumentLabel`, and a label naming a parameter declared at another position fails as `ArgumentLabelOutOfOrder`. Previously the label was dropped after parsing — the type checker paired argument `i` with parameter `i` and never read the label, and code generation destructured it away — so all three compiled silently and bound positionally. Against `fn subtract(left: i32, right: i32)`, `subtract(left: 10, 3)` was accepted although the language requires that when any argument is named all of them must be named; `subtract(wrong: 10, nonexistent: 3)` was accepted with labels that name nothing; and `subtract(right: 3, left: 10)` returned `-7` where the labelled reading of the source says `7`, with the emitted Rocq `.v` encoding the same positional binding, so a proof discharged over it was a proof about a program the source does not name. Labels select nothing — they are checked for agreement with the declaration and are still lowered positionally, so a labelled call is rejected rather than reordered. Unaffected: an unlabelled call, and a fully labelled call whose labels match the parameters in declaration order, both compile to byte-identical WASM; a parameter that binds no name (`_: i32`, or a bare type as in `external fn sub(i32, i32)`) may still be passed positionally; struct literals bind by name and may still be written in any field order. Migration: spell each label as the parameter it names, in declaration order, or drop the labels ([#378])
- A struct method whose `self`/`mut self` receiver is declared anywhere but the first parameter now fails type checking as `SelfReferenceNotFirstParameter`, naming the method and pointing at the receiver to move. Previously this compiled silently: every instance call pushes the receiver ahead of the written arguments, but the callee's WASM parameters kept source declaration order, so a receiver declared later bound to whatever value the corresponding argument slot carried while a user argument bound to the receiver pointer instead — a type confusion no diagnostic ever caught. Depending on what collided, the mis-lowered method returned a silently wrong result, trapped out-of-bounds when the mis-bound integer was large enough to be read as an address, or — when the swapped values differed in width (e.g. an `i64` field) — emitted a module that fails WebAssembly validation while `infc` still exited 0; the emitted Rocq `.v` artifact faithfully encoded the same broken ABI, so a proof built over it would be a proof about the mis-lowered program. Unaffected: a leading `self`/`mut self` still compiles to byte-identical WASM; standalone functions and `external fn` already rejected a `self` parameter in any position; `fn f(self, self)` is reported as a duplicate parameter rather than a misplaced receiver. Migration: move `self` (or `mut self`) to the front of the parameter list ([#377])
- A parameter name declared more than once in one function-like declaration now fails type checking as `DuplicateParameterName`, naming the repeated parameter and the declaration it repeats in and pointing at the repeat. It replaces `RegistrationFailed { kind: Variable }`, which reported the collision as a scope-registration failure in the symbol table's own wording, and it covers declarations that reporting never reached: an `external fn` binds its parameters into no scope, so `external fn e(x: i32, x: i32) -> i32;` previously type-checked, generated a module and exited 0. A repeated `self` receiver participates under the name `self`. One repeat now yields exactly one diagnostic instead of one per pass, and each further repeat of the same name is reported at its own declaration. Code generation is unchanged: its two parameter-collision assertions stay as backstops and now name a diagnostic the frontend actually has. Unaffected: `_: T` and bare positional types bind no name and may still repeat; a body `let`/`const` that reuses a parameter name is a different mistake and keeps its own report. Migration: rename or remove the repeated parameter ([#377])
- Proof-mode obligations now guard every universal slot with an explicit `HA_has_type (T_local i) T_i32`/`T_i64` antecedent (fused into a following `assume`'s antecedent when present), and `!=` no longer conjoins per-side `HA_defined` — aligning emitted hspecs with wasm-verifier's strictified `ValidSpec`, under which the old unguarded shapes were undischargeable (and, pre-hardening, vacuously provable). Downstream proofs over previously emitted obligation trees must be restated against the guarded shapes; wasm-verifier's `PrimeExample.v`/`with_spec.v` already prove them ([#353])
- A narrow-typed quantified scalar in a proof-mode obligation now states the **value domain its declaration admits** alongside the class its values ride in, the two grouped into one guard: `HA_and (HA_has_type (T_local i) T_i32) (HA_not (term_eq (T_relop T_i32 (Relop_i (ROI_lt SX_U)) (T_local i) (T_const (Vi32 256))) (T_const (Vi32 0))))` for a `u8`. Stating only the width was not merely incomplete: `ValidSpec` constrains its valuation in no way at all, so the slot ranged over every `i32` and a claim true of every `u8` — `assert(v <= 255)` — was a **false** theorem, while an existential narrow `@` could be witnessed by a value the compiled draw can never produce. The domain is the set code generation's own normalization of a draw produces (`emit_uzumaki_domain_constraint`, whose table this one is paired with row for row), so obligation and bytecode speak about the same values: `u8`/`u16` and `bool` and an enum tag take one **unsigned** upper bound (`<u 256`, `<u 65536`, `<u 2`, `<u N` for `N` variants) because their normalization zero-extends and a negative `i32` is already a huge unsigned value; `i8`/`i16` take a **signed pair** (`-128 <=s v` and `v <s 128`, likewise at 16 bits) because theirs sign-extends and the domain is two-sided; `i32`/`u32`/`i64`/`u64` admit every value of their class and are byte-identical to before. **The polarity belongs to the quantifier, not to the constraint**, and copying one position into the other is silently unsound: universally the domain joins the width guard as an `Himpl` antecedent, narrowing what the claim must cover, while existentially it is an `HA_and` conjunct *inside* the binder — `ktrue (HA_ex (Himpl g p))` unfolds to `HA_ex (Hor …)`, so an implication there would let a proof pick an out-of-domain witness, refute the guard, and discharge the obligation without ever reaching the claim. A constraint is attached only where the body actually reads its variable, which is what keeps an unread existential `@` collapsing to the vacuous shape **P010** rejects. Every position a narrow scalar can be introduced from is covered — a scalar `@`, a spec parameter, an anonymous `@` in call-argument position (whose domain comes from the callee's declared parameter type), a `forall` nested in an existential context (the `T_lvar` form, emitted inside its own `Hall`), and one bound per scalar leaf of an array or struct, at the element's or field's declared type, with a field's enum resolved where the struct was written rather than where it is read. An `exists`/`unique`-quantified **body** deliberately emits no bound: its choices are real parameters of the runs the reachability judgment quantifies rather than variables an assertion binds, code generation writes the normalized value back into the parameter a named choice lives in, and the judgment counts only runs that reduce normally — every payload-contributing statement compiles to `eqz; if unreachable` — so a false specification traps on every choice vector rather than being discharged by an out-of-domain one. New fatal proof-mode diagnostic **P015**: a `@`, parameter, or aggregate leaf at a **variantless enum** is refused rather than bounded, since an uninhabited type has no value for a claim to range over and no honest antecedent exists — `⊥` would discharge every claim over it for the wrong reason and any inhabited bound would be a lie (analysis rule `A009` already warns about the declaration itself, which only *warns*, so such a program did reach the translator). Downstream proofs over previously emitted narrow obligations must be restated against the bounded shapes; wasm-verifier's `Issue357NarrowDomainExample.v` discharges both the unsigned and the signed-pair forms with `Qed`, and stops closing if either bound is dropped or loosened by one. Unaffected: every committed `.v` and `.wasm` golden is byte-identical, since no golden fixture declares a narrow `@` or a narrow spec parameter, and compile mode has no obligations ([#357])
- `wasm-to-v` now fails closed on every construct outside the wasm-verifier proof contract, refusing each as `WasmToVError::UnsupportedFeature` naming the construct: all floating-point and SIMD instructions, every conversion naming a float on either side, `f32`/`f64`/`v128` as a value type in any position, and the unlowered proposal families. (The integer-to-integer width conversions were also refused here at the time; [#363] restored them.) Only foreign bytes via external linking (`infc -L` / `--wasm-dep` / `INFERENCE_WASM_LIB_PATH`) or `translate_bytes` are affected — they now fail at `infc` instead of at `coqc` or the prover; every `.v` the Inference corpus produces is unchanged ([#284])
- `core/wasm-linker` admits the eight integer width-changing operators an external `.wasm` compiled by a real toolchain actually contains: the three integer-to-integer width conversions (`i32.wrap_i64`, `i64.extend_i32_s`, `i64.extend_i32_u`) and the five sign-extension operators (`i32.extend8_s`, `i32.extend16_s`, `i64.extend8_s`, `i64.extend16_s`, `i64.extend32_s`). **This supersedes the two [#284] entries above and below it**, which retracted these same operators and stated the opposite; they are listed under Breaking, and this reversal is recorded alongside them rather than as an unrelated addition. Admitting a previously-rejected module is otherwise additive for linker consumers — what makes this entry breaking in its own right is two contract surfaces that change value: the public `SUPPORTED_WASM_FEATURES` const gains `SIGN_EXTENSION`, so an embedder asserting on it must update; and the vendored proof stub's `basic_instruction` gains a constructor (`BI_cvtop`) while `unop` gains `Unop_extend`, so any downstream Rocq development that matches exhaustively over either inductive stops compiling until it handles the new arm. All eight had been retracted under [#284] on the premise that the Rocq translator had no lowering for them; the translator now lowers each, so the "an allow-listed operator is one the translator can render" contract holds at the Rocq level rather than only at the Rust one. The two halves were refused in different places and are lifted in lockstep: the width conversions are MVP instructions gated only by the allow-list, while sign-extension is a post-MVP proposal the validator refused before any body was scanned, so `WasmFeatures::SIGN_EXTENSION` joins `SUPPORTED_WASM_FEATURES`. `SATURATING_FLOAT_TO_INT` deliberately stays out — its operands are floats, the proof contract declares no float number type, and admitting it would recreate exactly the allow-listed-but-unlowerable divergence this gate exists to close. Unaffected: Inference codegen emits none of the eight (it narrows sub-`i32` values with shifts and masks), so no `.wasm` or `.v` this compiler produces changes; only an external `.wasm` that previously failed to link is affected, and it now links ([#363])
- `inference_wasm_codegen::codegen()` now takes its configuration as one value: `codegen(typed_context, target, mode, opt_level, module_name, features)` → `codegen(typed_context, module_name, CodegenOptions { target, mode, opt_level, features })`; no behavior or emitted-byte change. Migration: wrap the four arguments in the struct or use `CodegenOptions::default()`; the two-argument `inference::codegen` wrapper is unchanged ([#315])
- `Inference.toml` is now strictly verified: every struct table (root, `[package]`, `[build]`, `[build.wasm-opt]`, `[verification]`, `[wasm-dependencies]` entries) rejects unknown keys instead of ignoring them silently; map-shaped tables whose keys are user data still accept arbitrary keys. Migration: fix typos and misplaced keys, and delete the removed `manifest_version`/`edition` keys from `[package]` ([#315])
- `inference_wasm_codegen::codegen()` gains a sixth parameter `features: EmitFeatures`; `EmitFeatures::default()` keeps output inside WebAssembly 1.0. Migration: five-argument embedders pass `EmitFeatures::default()`; the two-argument `inference::codegen` wrapper defaults it ([#315])
- Signed narrow division now traps on overflow: i8 `-128 / -1` and i16 `-32768 / -1` trap (`unreachable`) instead of silently wrapping to MIN, making division-overflow trapping uniform across all signed widths; `MIN % -1` stays `0`
- Exported functions now trap on an out-of-range enum tag: every enum-typed parameter of an exported function gets a prologue guard rejecting any host tag >= the variant count (a variantless-enum parameter traps on every host call); in-language callers never fire the guard
- Shift counts are now taken modulo the operand type's bit width: narrow (`i8`/`u8`/`i16`/`u16`) shifts mask the count (`& 7` / `& 15`) before the wasm shift, so `u8 x << 8` yields `x`; `i32`/`u32`/`i64`/`u64` shifts are byte-for-byte unchanged
- New analysis rule **A046** (`SpacedNegativeLiteral`, error) requires a unary minus applied to a numeric literal to be written glued to the digits: `- 42` and every other separated spelling (extra spaces, a newline, a comment in the gap) are rejected, in every position. `- 128` was never a literal — it is a negation of the bare `128`, which A022 measured on its own, so the same value compiled or failed on a space (`- 100` was accepted at `i8`, `- 128` was not, and every signed minimum was unreachable in that spelling). A022 now skips exactly the literals A046 claims, so it can no longer report a magnitude the author did not write; nothing is silently accepted, since every skipped literal is rejected by A046. `- x`, `- g()`, `-(128)`, `~ 5` and binary subtraction are unaffected. Migration: close the gap — write `-42` ([#376])
- New analysis rule **A045** (`FieldLessStructValue`, error) rejects values of a field-less struct — literals, `let`/`const` types, parameters, return types, struct fields, `self` receivers, through array nesting — fixing a compiler abort on `E { }` struct-literal lowering; the declaration and the `E::helper()` namespace idiom stay legal, and A011 is unchanged. Migration: give the struct a field, or drop `self` and keep it as a pure namespace ([#332])
- New analysis rule **A044** (`ShiftCountOutOfRange`, error) rejects a literal shift count that is negative or >= the operand type's bit width (e.g. `x << 32` on `i32`); const-declared counts are out of scope, as for the division-by-zero check and A022
- `inference-parser`'s re-exported `Input::new` now takes the source text alongside the tokens: `Input::new(&tokens)` → `Input::new(src, &tokens)`, so grammar rules can read a token's spelling. Migration: an external embedder constructing an `Input` must pass the string it lexed ([#219])
- `inference_wasm_codegen::CodegenOutput::spec_func_indices: Vec<u32>` → `spec_func_indices_by_spec: FxHashMap<String, Vec<u32>>`; the accessor renames to `spec_func_indices_by_spec()`. Migration: replace `Vec::new()` with `FxHashMap::default()` and `.spec_func_indices()` with `.spec_func_indices_by_spec()` ([issue#21])
- `inference::wasm_to_v` / `translate_bytes`: third parameter changed from `spec_func_indices: &[u32]` to `spec_funcs_by_spec: &FxHashMap<String, Vec<u32>>`. Migration: pass an `FxHashMap` (`FxHashMap::default()` for the empty case) ([issue#21])
- `inference::wasm_to_v` / `translate_bytes` gains a fourth parameter `hspecs_by_spec: &inference_hassert::HSpecMap`: pass `HSpecMap::default()` to source obligations from the embedded `inference.hspecs` custom section, or a populated map to override it; `CodegenOutput` gains a matching `hspecs()` accessor (empty in compile mode)
- Rocq output targets wasm-verifier on vanilla WasmCert-Coq v2.2.0, replacing the WasmCert-Coq-Essence fork: `ValidModule` is 1-ary and always emitted, the new `ValidSpec : module -> list hassert -> Prop` carries each spec's obligation as a translated `hassert` formula, and `spec` function bodies are omitted from the module record with indices remapped. Migration: downstream Rocq libraries must define the hassert-valued `ValidSpec` and update `ValidModule` consumers; theorems are `valid_<mod>` and `valid_<mod>__<SpecName>` (see `core/wasm-to-v/ROCQ_CONTRACT.md`) ([issue#17], [issue#21])
- New leaf crate `core/hassert` (`inference-hassert`): the `HAssert`/`HTerm` verification-obligation IR, smart constructors with `HA_true`-identity simplification, and the single codec for the `inference.hspecs` custom section (version 1, LEB128, hardened decoder), shared by `wasm-codegen`, `wasm-linker`, and `wasm-to-v`
- New fatal proof-mode diagnostics `P001`–`P009` (`core/wasm-codegen/src/hassert/`): a spec function that cannot be translated to a `hassert` obligation now aborts code generation (`CodegenError::UntranslatableSpec`) instead of silently emitting an unverifiable module; every diagnostic in a spec is collected before failing
- `wasm-to-v` rejects any non-deterministic instruction (`forall`/`exists`/`assume`/`unique`/uzumaki) in a surviving executable function body as `WasmToVError::UnsupportedFeature` — unreachable from Inference-compiled code, defense-in-depth for foreign `.wasm`; retires the vendored stub's `BI_unique` typecheck debt
- Lower `assert(<bool>)` to a WASM trap-on-false (`<cond>; i32.eqz; if; unreachable; end`) in both compile and proof modes, replacing a codegen panic; new golden fixture `tests/test_data/codegen/wasm/base/assert/` with wasmtime trap-identity coverage ([#195])
- WASM custom section name for the per-spec function index map is now `inference.spec_funcs`; external tools reading `metadata.code.inference.spec_funcs` (a misuse of the tool-conventions reserved namespace) must update ([issue#16])
- `inference.spec_funcs` payload now starts with a `varuint32` version byte (`1`); anyone parsing the section directly must update and reject unsupported versions ([issue#16])
- New analysis rule **A042** (`NonDetOutsideSpec`, error) rejects non-deterministic blocks (`forall`/`exists`/`assume`/`unique`, inline or as function-body modifier) outside a `spec` declaration, in both compile and proof modes. Migration: move the specification logic into a function inside a `spec`
- `&&` and `||` now short-circuit: the right operand is evaluated only when the left does not decide the result, so guard idioms like `x != 0 && 100 / x > 1` no longer trap, and a skipped right operand's traps and side effects no longer occur; lowered as a valued `if (result i32)` block, with new golden fixture `tests/test_data/codegen/wasm/short_circuit/`

### Changed

- The `SpaceWasm` remedy for an over-long import **field** name now names both edits: ``Rename the `external fn`, and the name in each `use { … }` clause that binds it, in every file that declares it: the field name an import carries is the declaration's own name.`` It previously offered ``Rename the `external fn`, or bind it to a shorter export field``, and Inference cannot express that second option at all — there is no rename-on-import, which is exactly why the field an import carries is the declaration's own name, and which is also why the rename is never a one-site edit: the same name is written again in each `use` clause that binds it, where a mismatch is refused as an undeclared import. It asks for every file because one import is one finding however many files declare it, so a reader told of one declaration would rename it and be refused again by the next. A reader who took the old advice wrote something unspellable; one who renamed only the declaration bought a second build failure. When the byte-level `check` finds either half of an import's name too long, the refusal additionally carries the linked-module branch the sibling remedies already had — ``If the import came from a linked module rather than from a declaration in this program, rebuild that module …`` — because that check reads an import section in which a name a linked module dragged in has no `external fn` behind it to rename; a refusal read off a program's declarations ends at the source edit, since a name read off a declaration always has one. *Migration*: a test or script matching the old remedy has to be updated ([#464])
- The Stellar export refusals for a **compound** and for a **64-bit** type, and the Val-ABI rewriter's surviving-import refusal, now point at issue **#324** where they pointed at #464, and say that this target refuses a host binding because the Soroban host-call convention is unbound *here*, where they said this toolchain binds no host functions. The old wording became false once host imports landed — this toolchain does bind them, at every target but this one — and the old issue number sent a reader to work that had already shipped instead of to the work that would lift the restriction. The clause naming the refusal is kept rather than replaced by the unbound convention alone: these two refusals tell an author that a compound or 64-bit value travels through host functions a contract imports, which reads as a next move, and it is one this target closes — without the clause the reader's own next build meets the host-import refusal instead. *Migration*: a test or script matching `#464` in a Stellar refusal, or either of the old clauses ``this toolchain binds none`` and ``host functions this toolchain does not bind yet``, has to be updated ([#324])
- Lift `infs`'s one-pass scan of a built `.wasm` (a leaked verification construct, bulk-memory use, the overflow-guard record) out of the `wasm-opt` command into new `apps/infs/src/artifact.rs`, with its tests, and the shared module builders into `apps/infs/src/testing.rs`; a second caller is coming — `infs run` must read an artifact's imports to refuse a program only an embedder can run — so the scan is now told which file it is reading instead of naming one caller's, and behavior and user-facing text are unchanged ([#464])
- Drop the never-populated `ExternOrigin::resolved_path`; the file a logical module resolves to travels with the linker driver's `ResolvedExternalModule.path`, so an `ExternOrigin` stays exactly what the source said ([#464])
- The per-external WebAssembly 1.0 gate in `infc` covers every target whose runtime stops at 1.0, through a new exhaustive `Target::requires_wasm1_externals()` — named for what it asks, and deliberately *not* `permits_bulk_memory()`, which asks what this compiler may emit. Emission is under this toolchain's control and a foreign artifact is not, so a target can narrow one without narrowing the other; what makes them answer alike today is that the linker is deliberately permissive about what an ordinary foreign toolchain emits. The refusal's prose is now target-neutral — "A contract is WebAssembly 1.0 throughout" was written for one target's artifact and would be wrong about a flight interpreter's — and still names the logical module, the file it resolved to, the validator's offset into *that* file, and the remedy. The opening renders the requested target as `--target` spells it, so it reads ``The `stellar` target: …`` where it read `Stellar target: …` before — the same move the three `codegen()` refusals made above, and for the same reason, since a reader pastes the name back. The Stellar export-admissibility refusals keep their fixed prose, which is reached at one target and has no variant name to hand back. Both e2e witnesses now assert their own opening, because one target asserting it is satisfied by a literal. `proof_artifact_refusal` is deliberately **not** generalized and says so beside itself: it refuses a *rewrite* that only one target performs, so `infc x.inf --target spacewasm --mode compile -v` keeps writing a `.v` that truthfully describes the `.wasm` beside it, and a reader generalizing the two Stellar-gated refusals in that file together would delete a legitimate build ([#325])

- `core/wasm-linker` merges an external's globals instead of rejecting the closure that uses them, so reading or writing a module global is no longer a Tier-C signal. The load-bearing part is the re-encoding: `global.get` / `global.set` previously fell into the operator rewriter's verbatim catch-all, so merging globals without remapping arms would have silently rebound every external `global.get 0` onto the main module's global 0 — which is `__stack_pointer` — producing a module that validates and is wrong. The neutralization confirms the shape of that failure: with the arm removed the link still succeeds, the module still validates, and global count, section presence and operator presence are all unchanged; only the operand distinguishes correct from miscompiled. Globals are deliberately **not** deduplicated the way signatures are, since two externals' structurally identical globals are distinct state, and an external contributes its globals only when its closure actually uses one, so an inert declaration stays dropped and every pre-existing merge stays byte-identical. The global remap is fallible like the type remap rather than infallible like the function remap, which is what makes the dropped space fail-safe: a closure that touches no global gets an empty remap, so a leaked `global.get` fails the lookup instead of silently rebinding. Two limits are recorded in `tier.rs` rather than smoothed over: a merged mutable global's initializer is a claim on a memory region that no parsed section describes, and an external that *returns* a global-derived value its caller dereferences is Tier A and never provenance-analyzed — the latter is not opened by this change and links identically with a bare address constant and no global at all ([#363])

- `core/wasm-linker` admits the scaled-index idiom (`base + index * elem_size` and its shift form) that every optimizing toolchain emits, and closes two soundness holes found while building it. The address lattice now tracks affine forms by coefficient **parity**: a value is parameter-derived when some coefficient is *odd*, because an odd coefficient is a unit modulo 2^N and the value is therefore a bijection in that parameter that no further arithmetic can flatten to a constant. Even-coefficient values (`p * 4`, `p << 2`) are `Scaled` — never an address on their own, but `odd + even = odd` is exactly the idiom. **Both holes admitted a fixed absolute address as parameter-derived.** `t = p; 32x (t = t + t)` needs no call and no recursion: it leaves `t == 2^32 * p == 0`, so a store at `t + 4096` writes address 4096 for every caller, and the module doc had listed `2p` as admitted *by design*. A root re-entered by an internal call with correlated arguments (`r(a, -a)`, with `-a` built from `i32.add` alone) fabricates the same thing across a call boundary; `Param + Param` now requires disjoint odd supports. Separately, exceeding a loop's fixpoint round cap was an over-*acceptance* rather than a rejection: the analysis broke out and used the state from before the unfinished descent. It now returns no verdict, and the cap is derived from the lattice height rather than assumed. The cancelling forms stay rejected and pinned (`i * 0`, `p & 0`, `p - p`, and the shift counts WebAssembly takes modulo 32/64). A differential harness executes each fixture under wasmtime with disjoint arguments and requires an accepted module's written byte-sets to be disjoint and a declared fabricator's to intersect, so no rejection fixture can go vacuous ([#363])

- `core/wasm-linker` warns when a merged external addresses linear memory through caller-supplied pointers and the reconciled memory exceeds one page. The merge proves every such address *derives from* a parameter of the call; it does not prove the address *stays within* the buffer that parameter points into, because the analysis carries no sizes — `p + q` and `2p` reach arbitrarily far with no constant at all and are deliberate accepts. A single fixed page was an accidental backstop for that gap: a reach past the caller's buffer left the memory and trapped. Above one page it lands in valid memory, possibly a region another merged module owns. The condition is deliberately keyed on the *reconciled* size rather than on a raised `[memory] pages`, because the backstop is gone whichever module enlarged the memory — a main configured to two pages and a memoryless main adopting a seventeen-page external memory are equally exposed. The warning states the limit of what the merge proves, not a fault found in the merged code; [#420] tracks the containment analysis that would close it. New `link_with_warnings`/`LinkOutput`/`LinkWarning` carry it, `link` keeps its signature as the warning-discarding form, and `infc` prints each to stderr. The reconciliation failure that rejects an external too large for the main module's memory now names the knob that fixes it ([#363])
- `core/wasm-linker` no longer drops a linked library's own proof obligations in silence.
