A library compiled with `--mode proof` ships an `inference.hspecs` section recording what its author proved about its own code, and only the executable closure of a satisfied import crosses the merge, so those obligations were never part of the output — the defect was that nothing said so, and a program author reading their `.v` had no way to learn that the library's `assert`s were absent. The external role returned `Ok(())` at the custom-section dispatch point without decoding either section and nothing else mentioned them, and `book/src/the-wasm-linker.md` recorded that as the contract, in those words. What the merge does with those sections is now an explicit input, `LinkOptions::external_specs`, with `Warn` as the default: it emits byte-for-byte what `Ignore` emits — the policy decides what is *said*, not what is written — and raises a `LinkWarning` naming each contributing library and both spellings of the opt-in. Under `Warn` and `Ignore` neither section is decoded, so a malformed one in a library nothing needed still cannot fail a link and the message states presence rather than a count it did not read; precision follows decoding. `infc` selects `Ignore` for a build that writes no `.v`, keyed on that rather than on the mode, because `--mode compile -v` is supported and does write a proof artifact that would otherwise have omitted the obligations silently. `link` and `link_with_warnings` keep their signatures as the defaulting wrappers over the new `link_with_options`, so the fuzz target and every existing caller are unchanged ([#362])

- The linear memory a build emits is configurable: an `[memory]` table in `Inference.toml` (`pages`, `stack-size`) and the `infc --memory-pages <N>` / `--stack-size <BYTES>` flags it forwards. Either key may be given alone and the other keeps its default, so a project that wants a larger memory need not restate the stack size; the two are nonetheless checked *jointly and after filling*, which is what lets a value legal on its own still be refused — `stack-size = 131072` with no `pages` is a 128 KiB stack in a 64 KiB memory. `--stack-size` sets both the `__stack_pointer` initializer and A036's budget from one value, so shrinking the stack tightens the call-depth diagnostic instead of leaving the rule policing a stack the artifact does not have; wiring only code generation would have shipped programs that overflow their own stack. `MemoryLayout`'s fields are now private with `MemoryLayout::resolve` as the sole checked constructor, so a layout that cannot be emitted has no representation outside `core/compiler-interface` — which is what let `codegen`'s validate-then-emit refusal go away rather than move. Compiler ABI minor bumped 2 → 3 to advertise the additive flags, and `infs` gates the forward on it: a project that declares no `[memory]` table forwards nothing and so still builds against an older `infc`. Every emitted artifact is byte-identical under the default layout, which all 212 golden `.wasm` files confirm ([#363])

- `core/wasm-linker` adopts an external's declared linear memory only when the external's closure actually uses memory. Previously every merged external's memory section was folded into the reconciliation unconditionally, so a module's declared page count became a fact about the merged output even when no merged body could observe it. The effect was twofold and both halves were wrong in the same way: against a memoryless main, a leaf `i32.add` over a 17-page `wasm32-unknown-unknown` artifact produced a merged module declaring 17 pages — recorded in the emitted `.wasm` and restated in the paired `.v` as the machine the proof is about, introduced by a pure function; and against the compiler's own `(memory 1 1)`, that same pure function was rejected outright with `IncompatibleMemory` over pages nothing would have touched. This **partly supersedes the [#363] tier entry above**, which stated that such an artifact clears the tier gate and then fails reconciliation: that remains true for a memory-*using* external and is no longer true for a pure one, which now links against a stock compiler main with main's single page kept as-is. Dropping the declaration is unobservable because `uses_memory` is closure-scoped, so when it is false no merged body contains a load, a store, `memory.copy`/`fill`/`init`, or `memory.size`/`memory.grow` — the last two being the ones worth naming, since they yield or extend a page count rather than addressing a byte and so read as unrelated to a memory's limits when they are in fact the operators that observe them most directly. Two guards deliberately stay outside the new gate: an unsupported memory *shape* (`memory64`, `shared`, a custom page size) is still rejected for every declared external memory, adopted or not, so the rejection stays absolute rather than becoming conditional on an effect flag; and a closure that uses memory when no module declares one is still rejected unchanged. Unaffected: the reconciler's refusal to relax a main module's pinned maximum is untouched, so a memory-using external over a multi-page module still fails against `(memory 1 1)` — configurable linear memory is a separate change ([#363])
- `core/wasm-linker` classifies an external's globals and tables by what its closure *uses*, not by what its module *declares*: the `!module.globals.is_empty()` and `!module.tables.is_empty()` disjuncts are gone from the Tier-C gate, leaving `effects.uses_globals` and `element_count > 0 || effects.uses_tables`. This is what makes a real `cargo build --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` artifact classifiable at all — every one carries an lld-synthesized `__stack_pointer` mutable global, and a `std` build also carries an empty `(table 1 1 funcref)` with no element segment and no `call_indirect`, pure linker boilerplate a leaf integer function never touches, which nonetheless rejected the whole link as `RequiresRelocatableBuild` over a declaration nothing read. It is **not sufficient on its own**: such an artifact declares a multi-page linear memory (16 pages is the usual lld default), and the merge's memory reconciliation never relaxes the anchor module's bound, so it now clears the tier gate and fails one step later with `IncompatibleMemory` ("the reconciled minimum (16 pages) exceeds the declared maximum (1 pages) of the memory it is merged into") against the compiler's fixed one-page `(memory 1 1)`. Configurable linear memory is a separate change. Dropping an admitted external's global or table from the merged output is sound because `ClosureEffects` is closure-scoped (accumulated over the bodies reachable from the root export), so a closure admitted with no global or table effect contains no operator naming either index space — which is what the relaxation depends on, since the rewrite pass remaps function and type indices only and the merge emits the main module's globals with no table section, so a surviving `global.get` would silently rebind to a main-module global and still pass post-merge validation. **Data segments deliberately stay declaration-gated**: an *active* segment writes linear memory at instantiation whether or not any instruction names it, so an unreferenced one still changes what the merged program observes (a *passive* segment would be inert on the same argument, but the parser retains only `data_count` and discards each segment's kind, so the two cannot be told apart). Still Tier C, unchanged: reading or writing a global, `call_indirect` and the other table operators, an element segment, a declared data segment, and `memory.init`/`data.drop`. The Tier-C reason strings change with the gate they describe, so each names what actually fired: "defines or accesses module globals" becomes "reads or writes module globals" (defining one is no longer a signal), and the single "uses a table / element segment (indirect calls)" splits into "performs an indirect call or otherwise names the table space" and "declares an element segment" — neither implies the other, and the shared string used to name a construct the module might not have. An embedder matching on the message text must update. Unaffected: Inference codegen emits no external modules, so no `.wasm` or `.v` this compiler produces changes; only an external `.wasm` that previously failed to link is affected ([#363])
- Integer literals are contextually typed: a literal takes the type of the position it appears in (annotated `let`/`const`, assignments, struct fields, array elements, call arguments, `return`, literal-built operands) instead of always `i32`; no coercion is introduced and emitted WASM for previously-accepted programs is byte-identical. Also collapses duplicate mismatch diagnostics, adds parser diagnostics for `16i64`/`1_000`/`0x1F` literal shapes, and A022 now names where a literal's type came from ([#219])
- Rebuild the type checker's scope tree as an index arena (`Vec<Scope>` keyed by `ScopeId(u32)`, no `Arc<RefCell>`), making `TypedContext` `Send + Sync`; behavior-preserving and golden-corpus byte-identical ([#157])
- Memoize `ide-db`'s per-entry analyses with Salsa 0.27 (a single tracked `analyze_entry` query) replacing the hand-rolled `FxHashMap`/generation-counter memo; the `Vfs` overlay stays outside Salsa storage and behavior is identical ([#157])
- `ide/ide`'s `Analysis` query surface now takes `&self` instead of `&mut self` (`AnalysisHost` wraps `RootDatabase` in a `RefCell`); write methods stay `&mut self` and behavior is identical ([#157])
- Cancel in-flight LSP analyses: a `didChange` supersedes older in-flight requests (`ContentModified`), `$/cancelRequest` answers `RequestCanceled`, and a cancelled analysis keeps the analysis cache; `apps/lsp` gains a router/worker split with strictly serial dispatch ([#157])
- Route `ide-db`'s analysis invalidation through Salsa dependency edges (per-file `FileStamp` inputs plus a conditional `AvailabilityEpoch`), reducing the write-path pass to editor-facing staleness bookkeeping; behavior-identical ([#157])
- Actually free evicted and closed entries' memoized analyses in `ide-db` via an evicted-flag sentinel swap, bounding resident analyses by open documents + `MAX_UNOPENED_ANALYSES` + a one-write-lagged transient ([#157], [#247])
- Serve read-only LSP feature requests (hover, definition, completion, documentSymbol, inlayHint) off the analysis worker on a two-thread read pool using per-request Salsa `Storage` snapshots; a superseding write answers `ContentModified` and quiesces live snapshots ([#292])
- Extract the shared project front end (import-closure walk, `FileLoader` seam, manifest source-root discovery) into new leaf crate `inference-project-model` (`core/project-model`); `core/inference` re-exports everything unchanged, and the IDE/LSP stack no longer links the WASM/Rocq backend ([#256])
- Drop the always-empty `ResilientProjectParse::warnings` field; the fail-fast `parse_project` keeps reporting `ProjectParse::warnings` ([#256])
- Document `RootDatabase`'s single-threaded, read-through-`&mut self` query model on `RootDatabase` and `ide/ide`'s `Analysis` ([#157], [#256])
- Declare `serde_json` in `[workspace.dependencies]` and inherit it in `apps/lsp`, `apps/infs`, and `tests` ([#256])
- codegen: the three function-body statement-descent passes now consult one block-classification helper (`nested_blocks`), so they can never disagree about which sub-blocks exist; emitted WASM is byte-identical ([#167])
- codegen: `Compiler::current_fn_name` mutable ambient state replaced by an explicit `fn_name: &str` parameter threaded through the statement walker to `lower_sret_return`; emitted WASM is byte-identical ([#172])

### Language

- The unit type has exactly one spelling, `()`, which is also its literal, and `unit` is a reserved word the parser refuses wherever a type or a name is written (see `### Breaking`). To code generation `fn f() -> ()` and an omitted return type are one declaration; proof mode's reachability specs still refuse any declared return type on an `exists` or `unique` spec function, `-> ()` included, where they accept the omitted one. The VS Code grammar scopes the word as reserved, and its keyword tests carry it
- **`checked(e)` and `wrapping(e)`**, two new prefix expression forms that fix how the arithmetic written inside `e` behaves on overflow. `checked(e)` makes every `+`, `-`, `*` and unary `-` between its own parentheses trap when the mathematical result leaves the operand type; `wrapping(e)` makes them compute it modulo two to the power of the width, which is what the bare WebAssembly operator does; an operator no annotation encloses traps instead. The expression has the type of `e`. The rule is lexical and it is about the parentheses: the forms nest innermost-first, so in `checked(a * wrapping(b + c))` the multiply traps and the addition wraps, and they reach nothing outside their own parentheses, so `wrapping(f(x))` annotates the call and leaves `f`'s body saying whatever its own source says. `/`, `%`, the shifts and the bitwise operators are never governed — signed division traps on its own at the one quotient a width cannot hold, `%` cannot overflow at any width, and a shift discards the bits it moves out by definition. The inner expression may be any scalar, integer or `bool`, because `checked(a + b > c)` is the comparison-of-sums shape an author writes at a boundary and the arithmetic being governed is inside it; a struct, an array, a unit value or a function has no arithmetic to govern and is a new type error, `ArithModeOnNonScalar`, whose note points at annotating the scalar leaves instead. A `const` initializer is an ordinary expression, so `const SCALE: i32 = wrapping(2147483647 + 1);` is how a wrapped constant is written. Both words are **reserved**, which is a source break: the positions it reaches are enumerated under Breaking above. The VS Code grammar and its keyword tests carry both words ([#316])
- File-based module hierarchy (Zig-style, no `mod` keyword): every `.inf` file under `src/` is an implicit namespace; `use a::b;` imports `src/a/b.inf`, `pub` gates cross-file visibility, `pub use` re-exports, and only the entry file's top-level `pub fn`s become WASM exports ([#63])
- `external fn` + `use { … } from <module>`: declare and call functions from external `.wasm` libraries via logical (platform-independent) module references; a separate link step (`inference-wasm-linker`) produces a single self-contained `.wasm` and `.v` ([#9])
- Add struct definition and parsing support ([#14])
- Add division operator (`/`) support ([#86])
- Add unary negation (`-`) and bitwise NOT (`~`) operators ([#86])
- Parse visibility modifiers (`pub`) for functions, structs, enums, and constants ([#86])

### Compiler

- `inference_parser::SyntaxKind` gains `UnitKw` beside `BoolKw`, shifting every later discriminant by one, and the type checker no longer registers `unit` as a builtin type name: `TypeInfoKind::NON_NUMERIC_BUILTINS` loses the entry, so `from_builtin_str("unit")` and the symbol table's `lookup_type("unit")` answer nothing. Every renderer that printed the unit type as `unit` prints `()` — `TypeInfoKind::as_builtin_str`, `SimpleTypeKind::as_str`, a function type's source-like spelling (`fn() -> ()`), editor hovers, completion details and inlay hints, the Stellar ABI rewrite's rendering of a return type, and the proof translation's P004 message for a unit term — and the linker's refusal of a unit `external fn` parameter now reads `` the unit type `()` has no value representation and cannot appear as an external function parameter ``, the phrase A049 already uses. The three arms that existed only because the name could arrive — code generation's value-type lowering, A007's return-type test and the external-signature lowering — are deleted rather than kept as dead defence, since no parse produces a type named `unit` any more. The checker's own capitalized `Display` of the type, `Unit`, is unchanged, as `Bool` beside it is
- `inference-compiler-interface` gains `HOST_SEGMENT`, the module-path segment that names the embedder, so `infs` refuses a `[wasm-dependencies]` key under the same spelling `infc` reserves rather than under a hand-copied one. The type checker, which does not depend on the contract crate, keeps its own constant beside the classifier that strips it, and a test in `infc` — the one crate that sees both — asserts the two are equal: the tests on either side spell `host` out, so a rename of one copy alone would otherwise pass every one of them ([#464])
- `inference-target-conformance` gains `spacewasm::check_host_imports(&[DeclaredImportFacts])`, which asks the three SpaceWasm **registration** caps — 31 bytes on each of the two import names, nine host-function parameters, and one result — of the imports a program *declares* rather than of the ones a finished module carries. The caps bound what an embedder can be handed, not what the decoder reads, so they are answerable before any bytes exist; answered there a refusal can name the `external fn` an author wrote, where the same finding read off an artifact can only name a two-level string in a module no file holds. Both entry points run one body, so a source-level refusal and a byte-level one cannot disagree about a single import, and one test pins the two verdicts equal; `check` over the finished bytes stays the backstop, since it is the only one that sees an import linking dragged in or a post-build step added. The refusal header follows the same split — findings read off declarations open ``the host imports <subject> declares cannot all be registered by a SpaceWasm embedder`` rather than calling the subject a module, of which at that point there is none — so `Violations::render` and its `Display` now choose an opening sentence instead of hardcoding one. *Unaffected*: every artifact-level refusal renders exactly as before, and no existing caller of the crate changes ([#464])
- **An `external fn` whose return type is written `unit` rather than `()` now lowers to no value, as code generation has always lowered it.** `unit` is a builtin type *name*, so the parser hands `-> unit` over as a named type while `-> ()` arrives as a simple type kind; the external-signature lowering read every named type as an `i32` pointer — which is right for a struct or enum — and so gave `-> unit` the result list `(i32)`, where code generation resolves the reserved name ahead of its struct lookup and emits none. It moves both kinds of extern. A **host** extern declared `-> unit` was refused outright, because the artifact check compares the two lowerings of one declaration and they disagreed: the build told its author their declaration contradicted itself, for a program with no external `.wasm` anywhere in it and nothing wrong. It now builds. A **linked** extern declared `-> unit` changes which libraries validate, and this is a behaviour change to `infc` and to `inference::wasm_link::resolve_external_modules`: the declaration is now checked against a library export that returns nothing and refused against one that returns a value, which is the reverse of what it did — and what it did was accept the library whose export disagreed with the import the artifact declares, leaving the merge to splice a value-returning body into a slot typed for none. A `unit` *parameter* is now refused in both spellings, which the driver's own documentation already claimed of it. *Unaffected*: every other named type, `Array` included, still lowers to an `i32` pointer, and no emitted module moves — code generation is what this lowering was brought into line with, and it did not change. *Migration*: an `external fn` declared `-> unit` against a library whose export really does return a value has to declare that value's type; no `external fn` in this repository declares `-> unit` ([#464])
- **A `use { f } from host::<module>;` binding now resolves without a file, and the artifact ships the import it emitted.** The external-module driver partitions a program's `external fn` bindings into host imports and linked modules. A host binding probes no search directory and reads no `.wasm` — the module string is what an embedder registers, not a path — and comes back as a declared `(module, field, signature)` triple instead of bytes, with no module to merge and no write-set contract to check, because a contract is checked against a merged body and none is merged. Its signature is still lowered, which is the one step that must not be skipped with the rest: lowering is the only refusal of a `unit` parameter on the extern path, and code generation's import registration would drop such an argument silently. Two files may describe one `(module, field)`, and they are held to one description of it — the lowered signature and the `mut` write set have to agree, since there is no `.wasm` to check either against and code generation would answer a disagreement by emitting *two* imports of one two-level name, which no embedder can satisfy; declared parameter *names* are deliberately not part of that agreement, because they take no part in the interning and cannot reach the artifact. Mixing the two kinds in one program is refused before any filesystem access — the linker's output has no import section, so a host import cannot survive a merge — and because a mixed program is likely to have a missing `.wasm` as well, that ordering is what keeps a `NotFound` from sending the author to fix a path when the clause is what has to change; the refusal lists every host import and every linked module, and states each way out with what taking it costs. At the link step a host program takes a second path: nothing is merged, the codegen bytes ship byte for byte, and the artifact's import section is first held to *equal* the declared set on `(module, field, params, results)` — which is what stands in for the linker's guarantee that no import survives it. An import no declaration covers, a declaration the artifact does not carry, a signature difference, an import that is not a function, one two-level name imported twice, and bytes that do not decode are each refused by name; a decode failure is never read as a module that imports nothing. Each of those six refusals opens `internal error:` and asks for a bug report, because none of them can be caused by anything an author wrote: every one is the driver's declaration walk disagreeing with code generation's import walk, two derivations of one set, and without the marker ``the compiled module imports `env`.`entropy`, which this program declares no host binding for`` sends a reader hunting their own source for an `entropy` that is not in it. The import reader behind those six now backs the byte-identical no-op path of **`link`, `link_with_warnings` and `link_with_options`** as well, which is keyed on the main module being import-free, so this is a behaviour change to those three named functions: it decodes the type section as well as the import section, so a module whose import section is well framed and whose type section is not answers *not* import-free and is handed to the linker, which reports what is wrong, where all three previously returned it unchanged. Strictly more fail-closed — nothing that was refused is now accepted, only a module that was waved through is now examined — and unreachable for bytes this compiler emits, since a module it wrote decodes; the one shape that moves is a caller's hand-built module ([#464])
- The linker's "could not resolve module" failure now carries two next steps, in the order a reader should try them. First the ordinary cause, which a bare list of probed paths names nowhere: the miss is almost always a misspelling or a search path nobody configured, so the message says which spellings have to agree — the file on disk, any `[wasm-dependencies]` key, any `--wasm-dep` name — or, where no directory was searched at all, how to configure one (`-L <dir>`, `--wasm-dep <name>=<path>`, or a `[wasm-dependencies]` entry; the branch previously named only the two flags). It leads because it is the likelier cause **and** because the clause below is the wrong fix for it, and no longer merely a failing one: a host binding now resolves with nothing probed, so an author who mistypes a linked module, reads as far as the first instruction and takes it ships the typo as an import and hears about it from the embedder. What the message deliberately does not do is re-spell the file it looked for: every entry of the probed list is already a whole candidate path — the resolver joins the relative file name onto each directory before probing — so a sentence reading ```crypto::sha256` is looked up as `crypto/sha256.wasm` under each location above``, printed under `lib/crypto/sha256.wasm`, would tell the reader that `lib/crypto/sha256.wasm/crypto/sha256.wasm` was probed. Then the reason a search path can be correct and the miss still real: the `external fn` is meant to be satisfied by the embedder at run time rather than by a `.wasm` file the build links in, and `use { … } from host::<module>;` is the clause that says so. The sentence is specialised on the shape of the name that missed — a single-segment miss is quoted back in full, as ``use { … } from host::<that name>;``, while a `::`-joined one teaches the form and leaves the module to the author, since no single host module can be inferred from a path — and both renderings carry the all-or-nothing rule, because a program's externs must today all be host imports or all be linked modules, so an author with a second extern who took the advice as written would land in the mixed-program refusal one build later. The resolver's only currency is a file, so without that sentence its list of probed paths sends an author who meant a host function hunting for a module that was never going to exist. The sentence lands in **both** renderings — under the list of locations searched, and under the note that no search directories were configured — since the second is the branch a user who never expected to name a file will actually see ([#325], [#464])
- New crate `inference-target-conformance` (`core/target-conformance`): whether a finished artifact is one its target's runtime can load, and what loading it costs. It is a **true leaf** — stock `wasmparser` and `thiserror`, nothing from this workspace — which is what lets `infc`, `infs` and the test suite all link it, and it enters the default build, the three clippy legs and the coverage leg through the `core/*` glob. `spacewasm::check(wasm)` answers two questions in one incremental validation pass: a verdict, and a measurement. The verdict is the WebAssembly 1.0 feature envelope plus the decode-time and registration-time maxima the interpreter imposes on the shapes a build can produce — parameter words, local words, call-frame words, a locals group, import module and field names, host arity and result count, custom section name bytes, memory pages — each constant carrying the upstream file, line and **unit** it was transcribed from at `spacewasm` 0.7.1. It is a claim about those and not a proof of decodability, so the crate's README classifies every variant of the interpreter's own `ValidationError`: modelled here, reached first by the WebAssembly 1.0 validation, decided by a host set the bytes do not carry, embedder-configured and therefore measured, or out of reach on this compiler's path — with nothing left unclassified, because a residue nobody wrote down is one nothing turns red about. Two further maxima are the interpreter's *compiled* form rather than a section field: `MAX_IR_INDEX` (65,535) is one 16-bit IR immediate, which a `call_indirect`'s type index and a `br_table`'s target count both have to fit — the target count through the interpreter's shared `instr_imm_8_or_16` emitter, the type index through a check written by hand beside its `write_16` — so one `IndexTooLarge` covers both and says which; no other index reaches *that* refusal, and the crate's constant records why for each (a defined global or function index is narrowed rather than refused and earns the `IndexTruncated` refusal below, a local index is a frame offset the frame cap bounds first, and a label index is refused as an invalid label before it can be one); and `MAX_BRANCH_UNWIND_WORDS` (255) is the operands one branch discards, in the same words the frame figure counts, measured at `br`, at `br_if` after its condition comes off, and at **every** target of a `br_table` including the default, each against the frame that target leaves. Leaving the function is not measured at all — a branch to its own outermost label is compiled as an early return, as is `return` — and a model that measured every branch alike would refuse a module the decoder runs. Those four are four of the interpreter's five measured unwinds; the fifth, the implicit branch an `else` emits over its own arm, is exempt with its reason written down: what stands above the `if` frame at an `else` is the block's result, and WebAssembly 1.0 admits at most one, so that unwind is at most two words. One refusal is not a `ValidationError` at all, and it is the only place this crate is deliberately **stricter** than the runtime it describes: 0.7.1 subtracts the imported count, bounds-checks the result against the defined table and then narrows a defined function's or a defined global's position to `u16` with an `as` cast and no check, so a module naming definition 65,536 or beyond *loads* and runs against the definition 65,536 below it. There is no decoder verdict to agree with there, only a wrong execution to inherit, and for a flight target an artifact that flies and calls the wrong function is worse than a build that fails — so `IndexTruncated` refuses it, its authority sentence says plainly that the decoder does not and names the upstream report (nasa/spacewasm#201), and its remedy splits on provenance while still naming the edit: split the module that carries the definitions. All five places that reach the two accessors are checked — a `call` operand, a `global.get` or `global.set` operand, an export descriptor, an element-segment entry and the `start` section — each with an oracle row of its own: the two export kinds are read by an arm each and the two global operators share one arm and one peak, six arms carrying seven references with a row each, so deleting the pattern that reads a reference — an arm, or one alternative of the shared arm's or-pattern — turns that reference's row red and no other reference's; a constant expression's `global.get` is not a sixth, because a module-local global is refused there as `InvalidConstantInstruction` before the cast is reached. The imported count is subtracted before the narrowing upstream and here, so the refusal reports the written index and the position separately, and one imported global moves the boundary from index 65,536 to index 65,537. The measurement is per function: control depth in frames (including the implicit function-body frame both decoders push), operand height in **values**, operand width in **words**, and the `2 + locals + operands` call frame. It cannot use `Validator::validate_all`: the two heights are instantaneous readings on a live `FuncValidator`, so the pass drives the operator loop by hand and samples after every operator, summing widths from the bottom of the live stack for the word figure and stopping at the first operand the validator no longer tracks — every slot after an `unreachable` — which contributes nothing, as does everything above it. That rule is the interpreter's own and had to be copied rather than chosen: the sum it produces is the figure the interpreter checks its own frame bound against, so rounding an untracked slot up to two words — which reads as the conservative choice — would refuse functions the decoder loads, and the oracle row that pins it is accepted by the real decoder on the boundary. A refusal renders as a header naming what was checked and what the caller did about it, then one block per finding giving both numbers, the **authority** that imposes the cap (a decode limit and an embedder's registration limit are met at different moments and shortened by different edits) and one thing to change; the six shapes this compiler cannot produce split their remedy on provenance instead of naming a source edit that does not exist — four of them share one sentence, and `IndexTooLarge` and `IndexTruncated` split the same way while still naming the edit, since for those there is one: a narrower instruction for the first, a smaller module for the second. The summary line and the over-budget warnings are rendered by `Report` rather than by each caller, because two programs print them — the compiler about the module it wrote and `infs` about the module an optimizer produced — and a build log whose two lines named their units differently would be worse than one line; they are unit-tested against hand-built reports on both sides of each reference bound, including the ranking that names the three worst functions. The refusal vocabulary is consolidated in the crate's `errors` module and re-exported from `spacewasm`, which is the path callers use; it is that target's alone, split out for length rather than because anything in it is target-neutral. The constant `MAX_FRAME_WORDS` exists because the oracle suite found it: the decoder bounds the whole call frame to 16 bits as well as the local size, so a function with an empty operand stack may declare 65,533 local words and not the 65,535 the local-size field would suggest — a checker enforcing only the documented field would have accepted two declarations the decoder refuses, which is the permissive direction and the one that turns a build-time refusal into a load failure in flight ([#325])

- `infc --target spacewasm` runs that check on the **post-link** bytes, after the Stellar rewrite branch and before the Rocq translation and every file write, so a refusal leaves no artifact; and on success prints one summary line on stdout beside `Codegen complete`: ``spacewasm: conformant with WebAssembly 1.0; deepest control nesting D in `f`, tallest operand stack V values in `g` (peak W stack words in `g`). Build the embedder with MAX_CONTROL_FRAMES >= D and MAX_STACK_DEPTH >= V (spacewasm_std uses 64 and 256); limits from spacewasm 0.7.1.`` Both maxima carry their unit because the two that sound alike are not the same quantity: `MAX_STACK_DEPTH` counts values whatever their width, while the engine's stack is in words where an `i64` counts two, and sizing either const generic from the other's number is exactly the deploy-time failure this check moves to build time. The line prints **unconditionally**, which is the deliberate asymmetry with `stellar_summary` — that one is withheld from a build that writes no file because it reports a file's size, and this one reports properties of the module, which a `--codegen` build has just as much. A maximum above the `spacewasm_std` reference configuration is a warning on stderr rather than a refusal, naming the three functions that reach highest, because a conformant module needing a bigger const generic is not a broken module. The placement is chosen for a future target that both rewrites and checks: SpaceWasm performs no rewrite, so here the two orderings are indistinguishable ([#325])

- `inference-compiler-interface` gains the requestable target name `spacewasm` (`TargetName::SpaceWasm`), the third entry of `TargetName::ALL`, with `abi_minor()` **7**. It is the first name whose artifact *is* the default's rather than a rewrite of it: nothing on the emission path reads a target, so a `spacewasm` build and a `wasm32` build of one source are the same file, and what the name selects is an acceptance envelope rather than a shape. Of the things a front end holding only a name can answer, that envelope makes `supports_proof_mode()` and `permits_bulk_memory()` both `false`. Three accessors are new beside them, exhaustive matches for the reason the predicates are — a name added to the vocabulary states its answer rather than inheriting one. `runs_under_a_plain_wasm_runtime()` is the question `infs run` asks before handing an artifact to `wasmtime`, and it has no emission-side counterpart, because whether a module can be *invoked* by a general-purpose runtime is a property of the calling convention its runtime imposes and code generation never sees one; it is `true` for `spacewasm`, which is what makes "non-default" and "not runnable" separate questions for the first time. `unforwarded_consequence()` and `proof_refusal_remediation()` supply the per-name clause two diagnostics were missing; each answers `Option<&'static str>`, so `None` is structurally "this name has nothing to add" rather than an empty string every caller has to remember not to append a space in front of, and neither clause names a selection surface, because both are appended to messages a flag and a manifest key each reach ([#325])
- New crate `inference-stellar-abi` (`core/stellar-abi`): the Val-ABI rewriter that turns a linked Inference module into an uploadable Soroban contract. `rewrite(wasm, exports, protocol)` takes the module bytes and the per-export source-type descriptor `codegen()` records, and returns the same module with one `Val` wrapper appended per exported function, the export section retargeted onto the wrappers, one function-name entry per wrapper, and the `contractenvmetav0` metadata section on the end. Emission is untouched: the pass runs after linking and does not run for Wasm32, so the bytes `codegen()` produces are the same for both targets and the marshalling layer sits outside the artifact a proof is about. Wrappers are appended **after** every existing function so no index moves, which is what keeps the `inference.checked` overflow-guard record byte-identical with no remap — and truthful, since a wrapper contains no guardable arithmetic. Exports are matched to descriptors **by name**, because the static-merge linker renumbers functions; the match is total in both directions and refuses either mismatch rather than shipping a method that is reachable and always broken, or a contract silently missing one. Every wrapper instruction sequence is the one measured against `soroban-env-host` 28.0.2 and recorded in `tests/tests/stellar/MEASURED_ABI.md`, including the boolean wrap's `i32.const 0; i32.ne` normalization, whose absence uploads cleanly and then fails every call that returns a truthy value other than exactly 1. The admissible set is `u32`, `i32` and `bool` parameters and those three or nothing as a return; everything outside it is a `StellarAbiError` variant naming the export and the offending element — a 64-bit or narrow integer, a struct, an array, an enum, a compound return, a method name that is empty, over 32 bytes, `__`-prefixed or outside `[A-Za-z0-9_]`, more than 32 parameters, a module exporting no function, a surviving import, a start section. The input and the result are both validated at the WebAssembly 1.0 feature set and the pass fails closed on either. The declared environment protocol is the named constant `STELLAR_ENV_PROTOCOL = 20` with `STELLAR_ENV_PRE_RELEASE = 0`, measured accepted by a protocol-28 host, carrying the rule that it must become the maximum introduction protocol of every imported host function once host imports are admitted at this target (#324). The crate is a workspace member through the `core/*` glob, so it enters the default build, the three clippy legs and the coverage leg; it depends on **stock** `wasmparser` 0.254.0 with default features off rather than the in-tree `inf-wasmparser` fork, which decodes the custom non-deterministic opcodes with no feature gate and therefore cannot testify that a module is WebAssembly 1.0 ([#324])
- `inference-compiler-interface` gains the compilation-target vocabulary the manifest and the command line share: `TargetName` (with `ALL`, `DEFAULT`, `as_str`, `from_name` and a per-name `abi_minor`), `TargetSource`, `TargetError`, `RESERVED_TARGET_NAMES`, `resolve_target` and `supported_targets_listing`. `inference-wasm-codegen`'s emission-side `Target` gains `ALL` and `as_str`, and a test maps every requestable name onto an emission target through an exhaustive match and asserts the two agree on the spelling — the emission enum is allowed to be the wider of the two, since a target reaches it when the emitter knows what to do with it and the shared vocabulary only when a user may ask for it, and without the cross-check the same runtime could enter both under two names. `Target::supports_proof_mode` now takes `self` rather than `&self`, matching `permits_bulk_memory`; method-call sites are unaffected ([#324])
- The bounds of the eight numeric types are now one table, `NumberType::range()` in `core/type-checker`, read by the three passes that ask whether a number fits a width: the literal-range analysis rule, the obligation translator's decision about whether a folded value is one the source names, and the new constant-overflow rule. Two of them carried a copy of the bounds and each decides whether a program compiles, so a width read one way in one pass and another way in the next was a single typo from letting the same constant be an error in one place and a value in the next. Behaviour is unchanged at every width, pinned at each end of each range by a test in each of the three crates and by one in `core/type-checker` deriving the bounds from the machine primitives ([#316])
- The static-merge linker reads, remaps and re-emits the `inference.checked` section, and refuses a link in which a reachability specification reaches a guarded merged body: new `LinkError::CheckedGuardUnderReachabilitySpec`, naming the specification function, the function it reaches and, where a library supplied it, the logical module it was bound under. An `exists`/`unique` obligation is a claim about *running* the retained body — the judgment fixes an entry vector first and only then lets the choices range — so a trap anywhere in the activation leaves no successful run at all and the claim becomes false rather than narrowing to the entries that avoid it; for `unique` it is worse than false, since shrinking the set of successful runs is how a claim of uniqueness comes to hold by accident. Code generation refuses the same reach for every callee whose body it can see (`P018`) and can never see an `external fn`'s, because it is handed a typed context while the dependency bytes arrive one phase later, here. **Every** guarded function of the output is a rejection, whichever module supplied it: narrowing to the library-supplied ones would rest on the program's own having been refused already, which holds on the compiler driver and not on the public `link`, whose main bytes never went through code generation — the two phases still do not double-report, because a library's closure reaching its own imports is already refused as needing a relocatable build, so a path from a specification to a guarded main body lies wholly inside the program. The walk itself follows every call edge whichever module the body came from, since a path into a library may run through the program's own functions. A `call_indirect` anywhere in the walked set makes the reachable set unbounded and the specification is then treated as reaching everything; nothing in this pipeline emits one, and an external whose closure names the table space is already Tier C. An `exists`/`unique` obligation whose own symbol resolves to no function of the merged module is a hard error rather than a skip, and the specification-prefix half of that resolution now lives in `inference-hassert` (`HFnRef::bare_in_spec`), read by both this walk and the proof translator, because a private copy that drifted would resolve nothing and make the whole check pass silently. Unlike the two obligation sections, this one is decoded under every role rather than only under adoption: it describes the bodies the merge splices in, not a library the output is not, so the caller's specification policy has no bearing on it. The merged section is what a *later* link reads — an artifact produced by one link and bound as a library by the next still reports the guard it absorbed — and a section naming a function its module does not have is refused at parse time, as is a module declaring the section twice, in any module and under every role: a last-wins assignment would discard the first section's guarded functions without a word and leave this check blind to exactly the bodies it exists to find, so the message names what would have been dropped rather than being framed around adoption, which would be wrong about this section ([#316])
- hassert: a `const` initializer inside an `exists` block no longer leaves its existential binder unwrapped — `const c: i32 = f(@);` aborted the compile in a debug build (`logical variable level 0 is not bound at depth 0`) and, in a release build, emitted a de Bruijn index naming no binder, which the codec and the printer both passed through into the `.v`. `const` now scopes its binders over the rest of the block exactly as a pure `let` does, an expression translated only for its diagnostics drops the binders it introduced instead of handing them to a later statement, and an out-of-scope level is now a hard failure rather than a `debug_assert!` compiled out of release ([#383])
- wasm-to-v: `translate_basic_operator` is now `todo!()`-free — all 285 unimplemented arms became grouped rejection arms, so an operator with no lowering yields a diagnostic instead of aborting the process ([#284])
- infc: the `UnsupportedFeature` message now explains the construct falls outside the subset the WasmCert proof model describes rather than being unfinished work, and points to building without `-v` ([#284])
- Compiler phases now run on an explicitly sized stack: `inference_parser::MIN_COMPILE_STACK` (128 MiB) states the requirement, new `inference::with_compiler_stack` provides it, and `infc` uses it, so deeply nested input no longer aborts ([#322])
- parser: `SyntaxNode`'s drop is now iterative, so discarding a deeply nested CST no longer overflows the stack; the derived `Clone`/`PartialEq`/`Debug` remain recursive ([#322])
- wasm-linker: new `core/wasm-linker` crate (`inference-wasm-linker`): `link(main_wasm, &[external_wasm])` statically merges satisfied imports' closures, rewrites index spaces, dedups function types, preserves the `name` section, and emits one unified binary ([#9])
- wasm-linker: reject external modules using floating-point (Inference has no float types and the Rocq translator models none), naming the exact opcode; sign-extension and saturating float-to-int are also removed from the supported set ([#9])
- wasm-linker: reject tail calls (`return_call`/`return_call_indirect`) and segment-indexed table ops (`table.init`/`elem.drop`/`table.copy`) with `UnsupportedConstruct` — the Rocq translator has no lowering for them ([#9])
- wasm-linker: main-module rebuild is fail-closed — start functions, non-function imports, table sections, and v128 value types are rejected with `UnsupportedConstruct` instead of silently dropped or mis-merged ([#9])
- wasm-linker: fix unsound Tier-B provenance rule — pointer subtraction preserves parameter-derivation only for `Param - Const`, closing a fabricated-absolute-address hole; also enforce the 256-level nesting cap and reject duplicate `inference.spec_funcs` sections, multi-memory mains, and trailing `spec_funcs` bytes ([#9])
- wasm-linker: merged external function names are module-prefixed in the name section (`mathlib::sum`, private callee `mathlib::#helper`, nameless fallback `mathlib::#func_<idx>`), collision-free across modules; the Rocq translator sanitizes every non-identifier byte to `_` ([#9], [#362])
- wasm-codegen: emit a WASM import section for `external fn` declarations — imports take the lowest function indices, local functions shift up by `N`, and extern calls lower to plain `call` ([#9])
- type-checker: `ExternOrigin { logical_module, export_field }` binds each `external fn` to its source module; `extern_origins()` on `SymbolTable` collects them for codegen ([#9])
- ast: Remove dead `OperatorKind::BitNot` variant — `~x` always parses as `UnaryOperatorKind::BitNot`; the binary variant was never produced by the AST builder ([#142])
- parser: Replace the `tree-sitter` + `tree-sitter-inference` front end with a resilient recursive-descent parser (new `inference-parser` crate, `core/parser`) producing byte-identical ASTs, collecting all syntax errors, and dropping the C toolchain requirement; `parse_external_module` moves to `inference::extern_prelude` ([#62])
- ast: Introduce `SimpleTypeKind` enum for primitive types, replacing string-based type matching ([#50])
- ast: Simplify Builder API to return `Arena` directly instead of using state machine pattern ([#50])
- ast: Add error collection in Builder with `collect_errors()` for better parse error reporting ([#50])
- ast: Add `@skip` macro annotation for enum variants without stable node IDs ([#50])
- type-checker: Add `type_kind_from_simple_type_kind()` for type-safe primitive type conversion ([#50])
- type-checker: Add type checking for unary negation (`-`) and bitwise NOT (`~`) operators ([#86])
- type-checker: Change expression inference to use immutable references ([#86])
- ast: Use atomic counter for deterministic node ID generation ([#86])
- type-checker: Add bidirectional type inference with scope-aware symbol table ([#54])
- type-checker: Implement import system with registration and resolution phases ([#54])
- type-checker: Add visibility handling for modules, structs, and enums ([#54])
- type-checker: Implement enum support with variant access validation ([#54])
- ast: Add `#[derive(Copy)]` to `Location` for efficient stack copies ([#69])
- ast: Replace `Vec<NodeRoute>` with `FxHashMap` for O(1) parent/children lookup ([#69])
- ast: Add `get_node_source()` and `find_source_file_for_node()` convenience API ([#69])
- ast: Implement arena-based AST with ID-based node references ([#25])
- ast: Add `NodeKind` support for AST node classification ([#25])

### Codegen

- **The Stellar rewrite now appends `contractspecv0` and `contractmetav0` to every contract, ahead of `contractenvmetav0`, which stays last.** `contractspecv0` is the XDR description of a contract's methods that `stellar contract invoke` reads to turn `-- add --a 2 --b 40` into typed `Val` arguments, and that a bindings generator reads to write a client; without it a caller had to encode every argument itself. Its body is one `SCSpecEntry` per exported method, in export order, each the `FunctionV0` arm: an empty doc string, the method name, one input per parameter — an empty doc string, the parameter's name exactly as the source spells it, a leading underscore included, and its type code — and the outputs. The codes are `SC_SPEC_TYPE_BOOL = 1`, `SC_SPEC_TYPE_U32 = 4` and `SC_SPEC_TYPE_I32 = 5`. A method returning nothing is described with an **empty** outputs vector, not with one holding `SC_SPEC_TYPE_VOID = 2`: that is what `soroban-sdk` writes and what the CLI expects, so `SC_SPEC_TYPE_VOID` is recorded among the constants and emitted by nothing. `contractmetav0` carries one `SCMetaV0` entry, key `infver`, whose value is the crate version the workspace declares (`CONTRACT_META_TOOLCHAIN_VERSION`, what `infs version` prints), which names a release only while the workspace version is moved with each one; the host does not need it, `stellar contract info meta` displays it, `stellar contract invoke` against a deployed contract and `stellar contract info interface` decode it — along with `contractspecv0` and `contractenvmetav0` — through `Spec::new` (`soroban-spec-tools` 28.0.0) and fail the whole command if any one of the three does not decode, and tooling reads the Rust SDK's spec-shaking key `rssdk_spec_shaking` from it (`soroban-spec` 28.0.0), whose second version lets tooling strip every user-defined type and event entry the data section does not mark. Function entries are always kept, so that key would strip nothing written today; it is never written all the same, because the marks are the Rust SDK's own mechanism, not part of the SEP-48 contract interface specification, and this toolchain writes none. Both are hand-encoded XDR in the crate's new `spec` module, every code recorded against `Stellar-contract-spec.x` or `Stellar-contract-meta.x` at stellar-xdr revision `9c9c145953e80990d6ff1ae3a6a973a0ce6d0694`, the revision the `stellar-xdr` 28.0.0 crate vendors and `soroban-env-host` 28.0.2 pins, and each section name against where the Soroban tooling spells it; the `add(a: u32, b: u32) -> u32` entry is pinned as the 60 bytes derived by hand from those definitions. The environment metadata section goes last, so every contract still ends with the same 32 measured bytes. The names of all three sections the rewrite writes — `SPEC_SECTION_NAME`, `CONTRACT_META_SECTION_NAME` and `ENV_META_SECTION_NAME` — are public, and so are `CONTRACT_META_TOOLCHAIN_VERSION` and `MAX_INPUT_NAME_BYTES` ([#466])
- **The Stellar rewriter refuses a parameter the contract spec cannot record, two parameters a caller cannot tell apart, and an input already carrying any of the three contract sections.** A caller reaches a contract method's parameter by its name (`stellar contract invoke` takes each argument as a `--<name>` flag), so `StellarAbiError::UnnamedParameter` refuses a parameter written `_` — or one a hand-built descriptor names with the empty string — which the descriptor records with no name, and `StellarAbiError::ParameterNameTooLong` refuses a name over 30 bytes (`MAX_INPUT_NAME_BYTES`), the XDR `string name<30>` bound of the spec's input-name field; each names the export and the parameter's one-based position, as `UnsupportedParameter` does, and the length refusal the name and its length too. `StellarAbiError::ParameterNamesCollide` refuses two parameters one of whose names is the other's `stellar` CLI flag alias, such as `x` beside `_x`, `X` or `x_`, or whose names are identical, which only a hand-built descriptor carries; it names both positions and both spellings, the earlier first, and the flag the two claim, and the pair reported is the earliest parameter that collides with a later one, beside the earliest such later one. The spec describes such a method correctly, but `stellar contract invoke` gives every parameter the flag `--<name>` and an alias, its name in `heck` 0.5.0's kebab case (read from `stellar-cli` 28.0.0's `build_custom_cmd`), and resolves a flag to whichever parameter claims it first, in the `HashMap` order it adds them, which changes from run to run, so a call through the flags `--help` lists may reach the wrong parameter or be refused; `_x` beside `__x` and `to_addr` beside `_to_addr` or `toAddr`, whose aliases agree but are neither one's name, keep a listed flag each and are admitted, and `tests/tests/stellar/MEASURED_ABI.md` records which of these pairs the CLI failed to call and which it called every time. Both gates compute the alias with the new `inference_wasm_codegen::stellar_cli_flag_alias`, a transcription of `heck`'s conversion, Unicode case rules and its word-final `Σ` included, that cannot differ from heck's on an ASCII name, which every Inference identifier is, and find the pair with the new `inference_wasm_codegen::stellar_flag_collision`. The three rules run after the type and return rules, each over every parameter before the next — `_` first, then length, then the pair — so a descriptor breaking two rules is refused for the earlier one wherever its parameters sit. `AlreadyAContract` now refuses `contractspecv0` and `contractmetav0` in the input as well as `contractenvmetav0`, naming the one it found: a second spec section would not be merely redundant, because a reader either takes the first `contractspecv0` it meets, as `soroban_spec::read::raw_from_wasm` (`soroban-spec` 28.0.0) does, or merges every copy, and neither describes the contract the rewrite produced ([#466])
- **`codegen()` now refuses a host import twice over: in a `proof` build at any target, and at any target that does not bind the host-call convention.** A host binding — `use { f } from host::<module>;` — leaves a function's body to the embedder, and both refusals are about what that means rather than about what a runtime can decode. In `proof` mode the body is outside the artifact the proof is written about and the translation has no way to state an assumption about it, so a proof build naming one is refused at every target rather than emitting a `.v` that quietly assumes the import away; the refusal names the `external fn` and the clause that binds it, and points at `--mode compile`. At the target level the question is the calling convention, and the new `Target::supports_host_imports()` is the exhaustive match that answers it: `wasm32` and `spacewasm` register a host against the WebAssembly signature the import declares, which is the convention an `external fn` already describes, and `stellar` does not — a Soroban contract reaches storage, ledger access and every host object through host functions the host defines, each taking and returning the host's 64-bit tagged word, and nothing in this toolchain maps an `external fn` onto one of them. The refusal says that, rather than describing the values the reader's own declaration would send across the boundary: what a target has not bound is true of `external fn commit();`, which declares no value in either position, as much as of a declaration with parameters and a return. So the target refuses the binding rather than emitting a contract that uploads and is wrong, and issue #324 is where binding the convention is tracked. The two ways out are ordered — remove the host binding, or else build for `wasm32` — because a reader who typed `--target stellar` wants an uploadable contract and the second way out gives that up. The predicate is deliberately **not** folded in with the four instruction-set predicates beside it: those split `Target::ALL` on `is_default`, and this one splits it neither way, since `spacewasm` narrows the instruction set and still binds a host. Both gates run **before** `emit()`, which is what decides the message a doubly-inadmissible program gets: a host-bound program whose exported signature the Stellar target also refuses — `external fn clock_ms() -> i64;` behind a `pub fn now() -> i64`, which is the corpus's own host fixture — is refused for the binding, because the binding is inadmissible whatever the exported signature turns out to be and is therefore the change its author has to make first. *Unaffected*: a compile-mode build at a target that binds a host — what an ordinary `wasm32` build is — asks nothing at all, since the bound externs are walked only where one of the two gates could fire, and no emitted module moves ([#464])
- **New code-generation target `inference_wasm_codegen::Target::SpaceWasm`**, for NASA JPL's SpaceWasm flight interpreter — a `no_std` WebAssembly 1.0 interpreter that decodes a module into its own IR on a fixed allocation and runs it with no operating system underneath. It is a third emission target, and the CLI change recorded under `### CLI` in this same release registers `spacewasm` as the `--target` name that requests it. Nothing about emission changes: the target narrows what a build may *contain*, and a module built for it is byte-for-byte the module a `wasm32` build of the same source produces, with no rewrite afterwards. That identity is now asserted corpus-wide for **every** non-default target rather than per target — over every single-file code-generation fixture, each side built at its own default optimization level, with a per-target floor on how many were actually compared and a committed negative control (`codegen::wasm::target_identity`). What the target refuses: `proof` mode; any post-MVP instruction family, because the decoder has not implemented bulk memory (`nasa/spacewasm#54`, with sign-extension and saturating truncation open beside it as `#55` and `#56` and costing nothing, since code generation emits neither family at any target); and a non-deterministic construct in a function that ships. `Target::default_opt_level()` is `Os` for it — size is the scarce resource on a flight computer — and `Target::ALL` is three entries. Its decode-time maxima (255 parameter words, 65,535 local words, 32-byte import names against a 31-byte host-registration cap, 9 host-function parameters, and the embedder-configured control-frame and operand-stack depths) are documented in the book and checked on every build by `inference-target-conformance`, recorded under `### Compiler` in this same release ([#325])
- The non-determinism refusal in `codegen()` is now a target predicate rather than a comparison against one variant, and the walk behind it is **total over nested definitions and within each one**. `Target::supports_non_det_functions()` decides it, joining `supports_proof_mode` and `permits_bulk_memory` — all three are exhaustive matches on `Target` now, as are both of the two `TargetName` carries (`supports_proof_mode` and `permits_bulk_memory`; the non-determinism question is emission-side only and has no `TargetName` copy), so a future target has to state each decision rather than inherit `false` from a `matches!` nobody revisits. The walk itself, `AstArena::first_non_det_def_deep` (new, in `core/ast`), descends a struct's methods; the gate previously looked at each source file's top-level `defs` only, so a `struct` whose method carried an uzumaki passed it. Analysis rules A006 and A042 both descend a struct's methods, so no `infc` build ever reached emission with one; a caller invoking `codegen()` without running analysis did, and that caller is exactly what this gate backstops. Within a definition it is total as well: `def_contains_non_det_anywhere`, the private walk behind it, descends every nested block of every kind, every statement variant and every operand of every expression, both matches exhaustive and wildcard-free so a node kind added to `Expr` or `Stmt` is a compile error there rather than a silent `false`. The shallow `def_is_non_det`/`stmt_is_non_det`/`expr_is_non_det` trio is untouched beside it, because proof-mode `hassert` translation is written against that reading and widening it would change what that pass sees; what the deep walk buys is that a `false` from it is a decision rather than an approximation, which is what a backstop against instructions a runtime cannot decode has to be. Before it, `return x + @;`, `f(@)`, `xs[@]`, `P { x: @ }` and a `forall` in a loop body all passed the gate. A042 and A006 remain the primary rejection and the only reading that reports a source location, so the gate stays a backstop rather than becoming a second diagnostic. Stellar shares the gate and its behaviour becomes total in the same way; measured over the single-file code-generation corpus, both targets' skip sets grew by the one fixture whose `forall` sits in a loop body — `SpaceWasm` compares 130 of 146 rather than 131, and Stellar 68 rather than 69, which is a measurement and not an inference, since that fixture declares no signature Stellar's export gate objects to. It deliberately does **not** descend `Def::Spec`: a specification body is non-deterministic by construction and compile mode strips it before emission, so descending would refuse every program that writes a specification at all. The one other definition it passes over is a module-scope `const`, which emission drops rather than lowering — a declaration that emits nothing cannot emit something forbidden — while a `const` written inside a body is a `Stmt::ConstDef` and is descended. The coverage mark renames a second time in one unreleased cycle, `wasm_codegen_stellar_rejects_nondet_function` → `wasm_codegen_target_rejects_nondet_function`, superseding the rename recorded in the `[#324]` bullet under `### Breaking`; a `cov_mark` name is matched across crates at run time, so a `check!` left on the old one panics rather than failing to compile ([#325])
- **Overflow guards** are emitted for every `+`, `-`, `*` and unary `-` whose effective arithmetic mode is checked, at every integer width and both signednesses, in Compile and Proof mode alike. There is no wider width to compute a candidate result in — an `i64` product has nowhere to be checked — so each row keeps its operands or its result in a scratch local and recomputes the question: signed `+` overflows iff `(r <s a) != (b <s 0)` and signed `-` iff `(r <s a) != (b >s 0)`, unsigned `+` iff `r <u b` and unsigned `-` iff `a <u b` tested before subtracting, unary `-` iff the result is the width's minimum, and a multiply is checked by dividing the product back by an operand. That round-trip has two inputs it must not be handed, `b == 0` and `(a, b) = (MIN, -1)`, so both arms are explicit and both come before any division runs — without the second the multiply would still refuse the overflow, but through WebAssembly's own `integer overflow` trap, which is a trap kind the Rocq contract's `BI_unreachable` role table does not describe. **Every guard traps through `unreachable` and through nothing else**, and the execution matrix asserts the trap *kind* rather than merely that a trap happened, which is the only assertion that can tell the two apart. The narrow widths are the cheap case: `i8`, `i16`, `u8` and `u16` compute in a promoted `i32` and are re-narrowed after every operation, so overflow is exactly "re-narrowing changes the result" — those rows run the crate's one re-narrowing helper themselves and leave its value behind, and the lowering site skips the re-narrowing it would otherwise emit next. Cost in instruction bytes over the same source with the operator written `wrapping(...)`: +13 narrow and unsigned add, +15 unsigned sub and `i32` neg, +20 `i64` neg, +23 signed add and sub, +30 unsigned mul, +52 and +57 signed mul at `i32` and `i64`, plus 2 bytes of locals declarations per width class. The scratch is one shared pool per function rather than a slot per guard, which the nested cases justify: an inner guard's live range closes before the enclosing guard's opens. It is reserved before the body is emitted, because `RegionEmit` hands out `i32` locals only and an `i64` guard needs `i64` ones, and it sits after the named locals, the frame-pointer temp and the two existing scratches, so every index those already handed out — and every `T_local` an obligation names — is where it was. The reservation asks the same classifier (`overflow_guard::guard_kind`) that emission asks, so a body cannot reach a guard whose scratch was never reserved. **An operator written `wrapping(...)` emits the instruction it emitted before guards existed and nothing else**, which is how each figure above is measured and how every regenerated golden artifact was checked: each was recompiled with the fallback mode set the other way and compared against its previous bytes, so every difference in the corpus is a guard and nothing else. Which operator `a + b` denotes is a property of the language rather than a setting, so the default is absent from the compiler's options, the CLI, the manifest and the `infs`↔`infc` ABI; it is equally not gated on the compilation mode, for the reason the emit-features invariant already gives — a proof about the emitted `.v` has to be a proof about the module that ships ([#316])
- Code generation now records which functions of a module trap on arithmetic overflow, in a new `inference.checked` custom WASM section: a versioned, strictly ascending list of function indices in the same instantiated space `inference.spec_funcs` uses. Its **absence** is the statement that no function of the module carries an Inference-emitted overflow guard, which is exactly true of a module produced by any other toolchain — none of them emits one — so a foreign `.wasm` with no section is read correctly rather than merely conservatively, and the section is therefore never written empty. Two corpus gates hold the record to what the emitter did rather than to a reading of the source: one over every committed golden artifact and one over every fixture recompiled in proof mode, one holding every committed artifact's section to being one the linker can read (non-empty, strictly ascending, naming only functions its own module declares) and one recompiling every fixture and comparing the emitted section against the compiler's own `guarded_functions()`, so the expectation comes from the emitter rather than from a predicate over the source text — `checked(f(x))` asks for a guard and governs none, and a fixture directory holding several sources would mark every golden in it. The list is recorded where the guard scratch is reserved, beside the `FnKey` set the proof-mode diagnostics read, so the index a linker acts on and the key a diagnostic names a callee by cannot come to describe different functions. No line of the emitted `.v` records any of this, deliberately: the discharge protocol hashes the `.v` text, so a header derived from the section would move the hash of every guarded module for no proof value ([#316])
- Three program shapes the front end accepts now compile instead of aborting the compiler. **`return;` in a function that returns nothing** was the highest-frequency of them: the parser synthesizes a unit literal for the missing expression, so every bare `return` in the language carried a construct with no lowering. The literal now lowers to nothing at all, which is what a value occupying no operand-stack slot should do, and **a bare `();` statement** compiles for the same reason. **A `_: T` parameter on a function with a body** now takes a real WebAssembly parameter slot and advances the slot counter, so every later parameter keeps the index declaration order gives it; it takes no `locals_map` entry, because nothing can read it and inventing a key would put a name in the map the source never wrote, and it earns no frame slot, because a compound parameter's entry copy exists so the body can read its own data and an unnamed one has no reads. At an export boundary it is normalized and enum-tag-guarded **by slot**, exactly as a named parameter is, because the WebAssembly ABI contract is a property of the slot rather than of the binding — `pub fn f(_: Color)` traps on a bad tag just as `pub fn f(c: Color)` does. The choice planner's arity count was corrected to match, since every declared parameter costs a frame slot and a suffix that skipped the unnamed ones would land the k-th choice on a declared parameter's slot. Every one of these aborted rather than emitting before, so no committed golden artifact contained one and none moves for this change ([#360])
- **`-> unit` as a return type** is a widening rather than a repair: `fn v() -> unit { return; }` did not compile at all before, because `unit` arrives as a nominal name that signature lowering treated as an unknown type and refused with `unsupported type in WASM codegen: unit` — a clean refusal of a program that should always have been accepted. It now means exactly what `-> ()` and an omitted return type mean, and no committed golden artifact carried the spelling, so none moves for this change ([#360])
- Code generation is now **fail-closed**: a construct that reaches it with no lowering is a refusal rather than a process abort. The body-lowering recursion is infallible — it emits into a buffer and returns nothing — so the only tool available at an arm with no lowering was `todo!()`, which aborted the process with exit code 101 and stock Rust panic text that `infs build` forwarded verbatim, on a program the user had just been told was valid. The compiler now carries a poison slot: such an arm records a typed `CodegenError` and the first one wins, the four entry points every other lowering path funnels through return immediately while it is set, and the body compilation takes the error ahead of the epilogue, the trailing `End` and the vanilla-contract assertion, so a truncated body is discarded rather than assembled. A new `CodegenError::UnsupportedConstruct` renders `Codegen failed: <construct> has no WebAssembly lowering; <rule> rejects it before code generation`, prefixed with a line and column wherever the refusal was made against a node rather than a bare type, and exits 1. No compile-mode diagnostic-code namespace is minted for it — `P0xx` belongs to proof-mode obligations, and a second namespace would give every shape a catalog entry a user can only reach by skipping analysis — so the `rule` field names the earlier owner in prose: an `A0xx` id, a family of them, or "the type checker". The backstops that remain are a string literal; a `string` or unit value in memory layout; a unit-typed parameter, a unit-typed `_` parameter and a unit-typed binding; a parameter declared by its type alone; a unit value returned from a function that declares a result; an uninitialized binding; a number literal carrying no recorded type, one whose text does not fit the width it is typed at, and one typed as something that is not a number; `Type::member` on a non-enum type; a field access on a type with no fields; the `**` operator, which has no WebAssembly instruction and no expansion of one; an assignment to a target that is not a variable, an array element or a field; a call whose callee resolves to no lowerable form; a compound-returning method or associated function called where nothing provides a destination to write the result into; a struct or array literal in a position that binds no variable; an `@` in a position that binds no variable, over a type with no value representation, or over a struct whose field is itself a struct; and a generic type in expression position, alongside which a function type node is refused as an unsupported type by signature lowering rather than through the poison slot, because the type helper it is reached through already returns a `Result`. Every one is reachable only from a library caller that drives code generation straight off a typed context without running analysis, or that ignores the diagnostics it was handed: on the command line the new rules and the existing ones reject each such program first. Output is unaffected — the guard is a branch on an `Option` that is `None` on every green path, and every committed `.wasm`, `.wat` and `.v` golden is byte-identical ([#360])
- Migration: **`fn f(i32)` on a function with a body now fails at analysis with A050**. It never compiled — it aborted the compiler at the code generation arm — so no working program changes behaviour; what changes is that the failure is a located diagnostic naming `_: i32` as the fix instead of a panic. `external fn` declarations keep the bare positional form and are unaffected. In the same way, a declaration typed `string`, `String` or `()` in a value position now fails at analysis with A048 or A049 where it previously reached a code generation abort, or, for a `string` signature, an `unsupported type in WASM codegen` refusal carrying no source location ([#360])
- Migration: **generic code that built green now fails at analysis with A051**. Unlike A048's, A049's and A050's shapes, not every program A051 rejects was already failing, and five families that compile today go red. A function declaring a type parameter that appears in no lowered type — `fn h T'(x: i32) -> i32`, `pub fn main T'() -> i32` — was emitted, exported and, in proof mode, shipped a complete Rocq obligation with the binder discarded; it was uncallable from Inference either way, because a binder absent from the parameter list can be inferred from nothing, so the fix is deleting the two characters that declare it. A generic declared inside a `spec` block compiled only because compile mode drops specification functions from the artifact; proof mode already failed on it. A type application below an array — `pub fn g(p: [Q i32'; 2])`, at any nesting depth — emitted a module WebAssembly validation *accepts* whose export reads `(param i32)`, because an array lowers to a pointer without its element ever being laid out. A type application on a field of a struct nothing uses, or on a parameter of an `external fn` no `use … from` binds, was never lowered and so was never refused — the unbound extern vanished from the module entirely, import section and all. And a type parameter that shadows a declared type was read as the parameter by the type checker and as the struct by code generation, so `struct T { x: i32; } fn g T'(a: T) -> T { return a; } pub fn f(n: i32) -> i32 { return g(n); }` exited 0 and wrote a module `wasm-tools validate` rejects; that family is a miscompile the rule closes rather than a working program it takes away. Every other shape A051 rejects already failed, at a code generation refusal that carried no source location ([#320])
- Two defects in how a declared signature's types are lowered to WebAssembly value types. **An array whose element has no value type** was lowered to an `i32` pointer without the element ever being asked about, so `pub fn g(p: [fn(i32) -> i32; 2])` exited 0 and exported an ordinary `(param i32)` addressing bytes nothing can size, while the un-arrayed `fn(i32) -> i32` was refused; an array is now a pointer only when its innermost element has a value type of its own, and the element's own refusal is what travels out, so every element kind is covered by construction rather than by a list that can fall behind — with one element kind refused in its own words, `[(); N]`, whose element answers "no value type" without an error of its own and leaves the array no stride. **An `external fn` whose declared signature names a struct or enum** was lowered from the entry file's scope rather than from the scope the declaration is written in, so a sibling file's `external fn probe(p: Point)` was refused with `unsupported type in WASM codegen: Point` — naming a type the program does declare — even though the `.wasm` it binds links and the linker's own signature check accepts it; the import signature is now lowered from the declaring file's scope, which is where the name was written ([#320])
- Fixed: **a struct whose field is a same-named struct in another file is no longer refused as a layout cycle**. The three struct-layout walks keyed their cycle-detection set on the bare struct name, which the type checker keeps only so code generation can re-qualify it and explicitly does not treat as an identity, so `struct Node { inner: lib::a::Node; }` was rejected with `cycle detected in struct layout for 'Node' -- the struct transitively contains itself` when there is no cycle — renaming either struct compiled the same program. The sets are now keyed on the defining-file-qualified identity, the same key the type checker resolves layouts under and the one the analysis-side twin of this walk already used, and the two walks that recorded the name before resolving it now resolve first, so an unresolvable name no longer occupies an identity. A genuine cycle is unaffected: the type checker rejects a self-referential struct ahead of layout, and the error still names the carrier's own spelling. No committed golden artifact changes — every program this admits previously failed to compile ([#320])
- Fixed: **a `spec` block's name written as the type of a struct field**. It is refused by no rule and canonicalized by the type checker into nothing — a `spec` denotes neither a struct nor an enum — so it reached the struct-layout walk as a name that walk could not resolve, and was reported as `struct 'Q' not found in type context -- the type checker should have caught this`: no source location, and an accusation against a phase that had just accepted the program.
